Headache is the most common symptom prompting evaluation for CM-I, but not all headaches in patients with radiographic tonsillar ectopia are caused by the malformation. The classic headache associated with CM-I is occipital or suboccipital, often exertional or provoked by Valsalva maneuvers (coughing, sneezing, straining, laughing, bending). These headaches are typically brief—seconds to minutes—though they can persist for hours in some cases. They are often described as sharp, intense, or pressure-like and localized to the occiput, sometimes radiating anteriorly to the vertex, behind the eyes, or down the neck.

Pathophysiology: Valsalva-associated headaches are thought to arise from transient increases in intracranial pressure and altered CSF flow at the foramen magnum caused by the cerebellar tonsils acting as a partial obstruction. During a Valsalva maneuver intrathoracic and intraspinal pressures rise, transmitting to the CSF compartment. If tonsillar descent impedes normal craniovertebral CSF flow, abrupt pressure gradients develop, producing rapid onset pain. In addition, traction or compression of pain-sensitive structures (dural sleeves, upper cervical nerve roots, and intracranial dura) may contribute. Structural lesions such as syringomyelia may also generate pain via central mechanisms, but the classic immediate Valsalva-triggered occipital headache most directly implicates CSF flow disturbance at the foramen magnum.

Clinical features that favor CM-I as the cause of headache include: headache onset temporally related to exertion or Valsalva maneuvers, occipital/suboccipital location, brief paroxysms or constant background occipital pain with superimposed paroxysms, and worsening with neck flexion or cough. Important distinguishing features compared to primary cough headache include the presence of other brainstem or cerebellar signs or imaging evidence of tonsillar descent >5 mm below the foramen magnum (though this threshold is not absolute). Migraine and tension-type headaches are common comorbidities and can coexist; however, migraine tends to be unilateral or bifrontal with throbbing quality, associated photophobia/phonophobia, and not strictly Valsalva-provoked.

Diagnostic approach: Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) of the brain and craniovertebral junction is the investigation of choice to evaluate tonsillar position, CSF flow dynamics (if cine-phase contrast MRI is available), and associated syringomyelia. The degree of tonsillar descent correlates poorly with symptom severity, so imaging must be interpreted in the clinical context. Dynamic testing such as cine MRI demonstrating impaired CSF flow at the foramen magnum strengthens the causal link between headaches and CM-I. Red flags that warrant urgent evaluation include rapidly progressive headaches, neurologic deterioration, and signs of raised intracranial pressure.

Management implications: The presence of classic Valsalva-associated occipital headaches together with MRI findings of significant tonsillar descent and/or CSF flow obstruction is an indication that decompressive surgery may relieve symptoms; conversely, isolated headaches without exertional or Valsalva trigger and with minimal descent often require exploration of alternative etiologies and conservative management.

# Lower cranial nerve symptoms: dysphagia, dysphonia, palatal weakness, tongue atrophy

Lower cranial nerve (CN IX–XII) dysfunction is a hallmark of brainstem and bulbospinal involvement in CM-I. Symptoms range from intermittent swallowing difficulty and hoarseness to persistent bulbar weakness with aspiration risk. The mechanisms include direct compression of the medulla and lower cranial nerve nuclei, distortion or traction of nerve roots, and ischemia from impaired regional perfusion. In addition, large tonsillar herniation or coexisting syrinx that extends cranially into the cervicomedullary junction can compromise motor pathways to bulbar musculature.

Dysphagia (swallowing difficulty) in CM-I may affect the oral, pharyngeal, or esophageal phases. Patients report coughing or choking with liquids, sensation of food sticking, nasal regurgitation (suggesting palatal dysfunction), and recurrent aspiration pneumonias in severe cases. On examination, impaired gag reflex, delayed or absent palatal elevation (uvula deviation toward the normal side), pooling of secretions, and cough weakness may be present. Fiberoptic endoscopic evaluation of swallowing (FEES) or videofluoroscopic swallow study is often necessary to characterize the phase(s) involved, detect silent aspiration, and guide management. Aspiration risk is an important decision point: objective evidence of aspiration or significant penetration generally prompts more urgent consideration of interventions to relieve medullary compression.

Dysphonia and altered voice quality reflect dysfunction of the recurrent laryngeal nerve or nucleus ambiguus. Patients may present with hoarseness, breathy voice, diminished cough strength, and vocal fatigue. Laryngoscopy may reveal unilateral or bilateral vocal cord paresis. Palatal weakness and velopharyngeal insufficiency result in nasality and nasal regurgitation during swallowing. Palatal asymmetry may be subtle and is elicited by asking the patient to say "ah" while observing elevation and symmetry of the soft palate.

Hypoglossal nerve (CN XII) involvement causes tongue weakness and atrophy. Patients may complain of dysarthria, difficulty manipulating food, and drooling. Examination may show tongue fasciculations, deviation toward the weak side on protrusion, and muscle wasting in chronic cases. Tongue atrophy and fasciculations should prompt consideration of alternative diagnoses (e.g., motor neuron disease), but in a patient with CM-I and compatible medullary compression they can be attributable to chronic hypoglossal nucleus or nerve root compromise.

Diagnostic testing: Beyond MRI of the craniovertebral junction, targeted studies include laryngoscopy, FEES, videofluoroscopic swallow studies, and, when needed, electromyography (EMG) of bulbar muscles to distinguish lower motor neuron from neuromuscular junction or myopathic causes. Pulmonary function testing and aspiration risk assessment (e.g., oxygenation, frequent pneumonias) help determine urgency. It is important to evaluate for confounders such as prior cervical spine surgery, radiotherapy, or ENT pathology.

Clinical reasoning and red flags: New-onset or progressive bulbar symptoms in CM-I warrant careful assessment because of aspiration risk and potential for rapid deterioration. Asymmetric cranial nerve findings, isolated hypoglossal wasting without other signs of brainstem involvement, or rapidly progressive bulbar palsy should prompt consideration of alternative or additional pathology (neoplasm, motor neuron disease, inflammatory processes) and appropriate referral.

# Cerebellar signs and ataxia

Cerebellar dysfunction is a natural consequence when cerebellar tonsils are displaced or when cerebellar hemispheres and vermis are compromised secondary to crowding at the foramen magnum. Manifestations include gait ataxia, limb dysmetria, dysdiadochokinesia, truncal instability, intention tremor, and dysarthria of ataxic quality. Signs may be subtle early in the disease and become more apparent with progression or with superimposed syringomyelia that disrupts cerebellar pathways.

Pathophysiology: Direct mechanical deformation of cerebellar tissue alters local circuitry and efferent/afferent pathway conduction. In addition, compromised CSF flow and pressure gradients can cause secondary injury and demyelination of cerebellar afferents and spinocerebellar tracts. When a syrinx extends into the cervical cord, involvement of spinocerebellar tracts contributes to limb ataxia and proprioceptive deficits. The vermis and cerebellar tonsils are particularly relevant to truncal stability and gait, and their descent or compression often correlates with gait disturbance.

Clinical features: Gait ataxia is often the most functionally consequential sign. Patients describe unsteady gait, difficulty with tandem walking, frequent falls, or a wide-based, unsteady gait. Limb ataxia manifests as dysmetria on finger-nose and heel-shin testing and as impaired rapid alternating movements. Truncal ataxia may present as titubation or marked instability when sitting or standing without moving the limbs. Cerebellar dysarthria is characterized by irregular speech rate, scanning quality, and variable articulation.

Examination pitfalls and differentiation: It is critical to distinguish cerebellar ataxia from sensory ataxia (due to dorsal column dysfunction). Sensory ataxia is accompanied by impaired joint position and vibration sense and is worse with eyes closed (positive Romberg sign). In CM-I, concomitant dorsal column involvement can occur due to syringomyelia; thus a mixed picture is common. Coordination testing should be performed in all planes and contexts, and proprioception assessed separately. In addition, extrapyramidal signs or peripheral neuropathy can confound interpretation.

Investigations: MRI of the brain and entire spinal cord is essential to evaluate the extent of tonsillar herniation, compression of cerebellar tissue, and presence or size of syringomyelia. Neurophysiological testing (somatosensory evoked potentials) may document long-tract dysfunction. Quantifying functional impairment (gait speed, timed up-and-go) can help monitor progression and guide management decisions.

Clinical reasoning: The presence of progressive cerebellar signs, particularly when associated with objective imaging correlates (compression, syrinx), often indicates more advanced disease and prompts consideration of decompression. Isolated mild ataxia without imaging evidence may not be attributable to CM-I alone and should prompt evaluation for alternative etiologies (alcohol, medication effects, peripheral neuropathy, degenerative cerebellar disease).

# Sleep-related breathing disorders and daytime respiratory symptoms

Sleep-disordered breathing (SDB) is an underappreciated but clinically significant manifestation of CM-I. Disorders include central sleep apnea (CSA), obstructive sleep apnea (OSA), hypoventilation, and sleep-related hypoxemia. Daytime respiratory symptoms such as dyspnea, chronic cough, and orthopnea can also occur. The mechanisms involve brainstem compression of respiratory centers (dorsal and ventral respiratory groups, nucleus tractus solitarius), impairment of cranial nerve function controlling upper airway tone (vagal and hypoglossal involvement), and autonomic dysfunction affecting respiratory drive.

Central sleep apnea: CSA arises when brainstem respiratory rhythm generators and their afferent inputs are disrupted. In CM-I, compression of the medulla or impaired perfusion can blunt chemoreceptor responsiveness to hypercapnia and hypoxia, leading to periods of absent respiratory effort during sleep. Clinically, CSA presents with witnessed apneas without respiratory effort, excessive daytime sleepiness, morning headaches, and fragmented sleep.

Obstructive sleep apnea and upper airway instability: Damage to hypoglossal and vagal motor pathways can reduce tone of the genioglossus and other upper airway dilator muscles, predisposing to collapse during sleep and OSA. Palatal weakness and velopharyngeal dysfunction further compromise airway patency. Patients present with snoring, witnessed apneas, daytime somnolence, and cognitive complaints.

Hypoventilation and respiratory failure: In severe cases, impaired central drive or bulbar weakness can produce hypoventilation with daytime hypercapnia, orthopnea, and recurrent respiratory infections. Pulmonary function testing may show reduced vital capacity (particularly when supine), and blood gas analysis can reveal elevated PaCO2. Normal PaCO2 is approximately 35–45 mm Hg. Daytime hypercapnia (PaCO2 >45 mm Hg) is concerning and may indicate advanced brainstem dysfunction with imminent respiratory decompensation.

Diagnostic evaluation: Polysomnography (full overnight sleep study) is the gold standard to characterize SDB, distinguishing CSA from OSA and quantifying severity (apnea-hypopnea index, oxygen desaturation indices). Arterial blood gas testing and capnography can detect hypoventilation. Pulmonary function tests, with supine measures, assess diaphragmatic and ventilatory muscle strength. MRI of the brainstem and cervical cord assesses degree of compression correlating with respiratory center involvement.

Clinical importance and decision points: SDB in CM-I can be life-threatening. The detection of CSA, significant OSA with bulbar weakness, progressive daytime hypercapnia, or declining vital capacity warrants prompt neurosurgical and respiratory referral. Even mild sleep apnea can contribute to daytime fatigue and cognitive dysfunction, so screening (via questionnaires and consideration of polysomnography) is appropriate for symptomatic patients or those with medullary signs.

# Pain syndromes including occipital neuralgia and scalp allodynia

Beyond the classic occipital Valsalva headache, CM-I can produce multiple pain syndromes attributable to upper cervical nerve root irritation, meningeal traction, and central sensitization. Two commonly described syndromes are occipital neuralgia and scalp allodynia; both can be debilitating and may persist despite anatomical decompression in some patients.

Occipital neuralgia: This syndrome involves paroxysmal, lancinating pain in the distribution of the greater occipital nerve (C2) and less commonly the lesser occipital nerve (C2–C3). Pain is often unilateral but can be bilateral, described as sharp, shooting, or electric-shock like, and may be triggered by neck movement or provocation of the affected nerve. In CM-I, altered tonsillar position and mechanical crowding at the foramen magnum can irritate upper cervical nerve roots or their dural sleeves, producing occipital neuralgia. On exam, tenderness over the occipital protuberance, reproduction of pain by pressure in the occipital area, and relief with anesthetic block of the greater occipital nerve support the diagnosis.

Scalp allodynia and central sensitization: Some patients develop a persistent burning or hypersensitivity of the scalp, where light touch or combing hair provokes pain (allodynia). This can reflect central sensitization within trigeminocervical convergence circuits or chronic irritation of dural afferents and upper cervical inputs. Scalp allodynia may be associated with migraine comorbidity and can be challenging to treat.

Pathophysiology: Peripheral nociceptive input from obstructed CSF flow, meningeal stretch, and cervical root irritation can lead to sensitization of second-order neurons in the trigeminocervical complex, perpetuating pain beyond the initial mechanical stimulus. Secondary changes in descending inhibitory pathways may maintain chronicity.

Diagnosis and management considerations: Careful history distinguishes occipital neuralgia from cervicogenic headache and migraine. Response to diagnostic greater occipital nerve block is both diagnostic and therapeutic. Imaging is necessary to identify CM-I and concomitant structural lesions. When occipital neuralgia coexists with CM-I, decompressive surgery may relieve pain in patients whose headaches are clearly Valsalva-related or who have objective dorsal compression. For persistent neuralgic pain, targeted therapies including nerve blocks, local injections, neuromodulation, and neuropathic pain medications may be required.

# Prevalence, natural history and demographic patterns of symptomatic disease

Prevalence estimates for CM-I vary with the modality and population studied. Radiographic tonsillar ectopia is increasingly identified incidentally due to widespread MRI use. Population-based MRI studies suggest radiographic CM-I (commonly defined as tonsillar descent of ≥5 mm) occurs in approximately 0.24% to 0.9% of individuals. However, the proportion of individuals with radiographic CM-I who are symptomatic is much smaller; symptomatic CM-I prevalence is estimated at roughly 0.01% to 0.05% depending on diagnostic criteria and referral patterns.

Demographic patterns: Symptomatic CM-I occurs across the lifespan but most commonly presents in adolescents and young to middle-aged adults. There is a slight female predominance in many clinical series, particularly among symptomatic patients, though some imaging-based prevalence studies show more balanced sex distribution. Presentation in childhood often includes scoliosis or syringomyelia-related signs, whereas adults more commonly present with headaches, cranial nerve symptoms, or progressive gait disturbance.

Natural history: The course of CM-I is heterogeneous. Many individuals with radiographic tonsillar descent remain asymptomatic or have stable, mild symptoms for years. Symptom onset can be insidious or precipitated by minor trauma, rapid growth in adolescence, or events that alter CSF dynamics. Progressive decline with worsening headache frequency, new brainstem signs, development of syringomyelia, progressive scoliosis (in children), or respiratory compromise represents a more serious trajectory. Factors that favor progression include significant obstruction of CSF flow on cine MRI, presence and expansion of a syrinx, and progressive neurological deficits on serial examinations.

Long-term outcomes: In patients carefully selected for decompressive surgery (those with clear symptoms attributable to CM-I and imaging correlates), many demonstrate symptomatic improvement, particularly in Valsalva headaches and some cranial nerve and gait symptoms. However, headaches not conforming to the Valsalva-exertional phenotype and chronic neuropathic pain syndromes are less predictably responsive. Surveillance strategies for asymptomatic patients typically involve clinical monitoring and repeat imaging if new symptoms occur. For patients with small, asymptomatic tonsillar ectopia, routine imaging intervals are individualized based on symptoms, age, and presence of syrinx.

Clinical decision points and epidemiologic considerations: Because many people harbor incidental tonsillar descent, clinical attribution requires correlation between history, neurologic findings, and imaging. Identifying red flags—progressive neurologic deficits, increasing syrinx size, new or worsening bulbar or respiratory symptoms, or severe exertional headaches—guides the urgency of referral and intervention. Epidemiologic awareness helps avoid overdiagnosis and overtreatment: the mere presence of tonsillar ectopia on MRI does not mandate neurosurgical intervention without consistent clinical evidence of CM-I–related dysfunction.

Conclusion

Chiari I malformation produces a spectrum of clinical manifestations that reflect impeded CSF flow, direct compression/traction of neural structures, and secondary spinal cord pathology. Recognizing the characteristic Valsalva-associated occipital headache, lower cranial nerve dysfunction, cerebellar ataxia, sleep-related breathing disorders, and pain syndromes such as occipital neuralgia—and appreciating the epidemiologic context and natural history—are essential for attributing symptoms correctly, selecting appropriate diagnostic testing (MRI with cine flow studies, polysomnography, swallow studies), assessing urgency, and guiding management decisions. Clinical correlation is paramount given the imperfect relationship between anatomical descent and symptom burden; a careful, multidisciplinary approach optimizes outcomes and minimizes unnecessary interventions.

Introduction

Sleep-related breathing disorders comprise a spectrum of conditions in which abnormal respiratory patterns during sleep lead to disturbed gas exchange, recurrent sleep fragmentation, and downstream multisystem consequences. Within this spectrum, sleep apnoea syndromes are broadly categorised as obstructive, central, or mixed. Central sleep apnoea (CSA) and sleep-related hypoventilation due to failure of automatic brainstem respiratory drive represent a distinct pathophysiologic and clinical group from the much more common obstructive sleep apnoea (OSA). Understanding the mechanistic differences, the characteristic polysomnographic patterns, and the systemic consequences of central disorders of breathing is essential for accurate diagnosis and appropriate management. This chapter will define and contrast obstructive and central events, delve into the pathophysiology of central apnoea (including ventilatory control instability and loss of automatic drive), describe Cheyne–Stokes respiration and related central patterns, examine the effects of central apnoea on gas exchange, sleep architecture and cardiovascular risk, review causes of acquired central apnoea—emphasising structural brainstem lesions—and outline clinical features that distinguish central from obstructive events.

# Distinction between obstructive, central and mixed sleep apnoea

Sleep apnoea describes repetitive episodes of cessation (apnoea) or reduction (hypopnoea) of airflow during sleep. The key categorisation is based on the presence or absence of ventilatory effort during these events. Obstructive sleep apnoea (OSA) is characterised by continued or increased respiratory effort against an occluded or markedly narrowed upper airway; central sleep apnoea (CSA) is defined by absent or near-absent respiratory effort, reflecting transient failure of central respiratory motor output; mixed apnoea begins as central (no effort) and transitions to obstructive (resumption of effort against a blocked airway) within the same event.

Clinically and diagnostically, the distinction is made via polysomnography (PSG) that records airflow, respiratory effort (thoracic and abdominal inductance plethysmography or esophageal pressure), oxygen saturation, and often capnography. In OSA, episodes present with reduced or absent airflow while thoracoabdominal effort persists or paradoxically increases—esophageal pressure swings and inspiratory effort-related arousals are common. In CSA, both airflow and effort cease concurrently; there is little to no diaphragmatic or accessory muscle contraction during the apnoeic interval. Mixed apnoeas show an initial absence of effort followed by the onset of effort without restoration of airflow until the obstructive component resolves, often with an arousal.

Diagnostic criteria rely on the apnoea–hypopnoea index (AHI): apnoea is typically defined as a ≥90% reduction in airflow lasting at least 10 seconds; hypopnoea definitions vary but often use a ≥30% airflow reduction with ≥3%–4% oxygen desaturation or an arousal. The American Academy of Sleep Medicine (AASM) recommends scoring central apnoea when there is a clear absence of inspiratory effort throughout the event. Mixed apnoeas are scored when a central component is followed by resumption of effort without airflow. Clinically, mixed events suggest a combination of upper airway vulnerability and central instability; they commonly occur in advanced OSA or in settings of opioid use or heart failure.

Understanding the distinction affects management. OSA primarily responds to therapies that stabilise the upper airway (continuous positive airway pressure, mandibular advancement devices, weight loss, upper airway surgery), whereas CSA management targets ventilatory control mechanisms, addressing underlying causes (heart failure optimisation, withdrawal of offending medications) and, when indicated, noninvasive ventilation strategies such as adaptive servo-ventilation (with important contraindications), supplemental oxygen, or acetazolamide. Mixed apnoea may require combined approaches.

# Pathophysiology of central sleep apnoea: ventilatory control instability and failure of automatic drive

The pathophysiology of central sleep apnoea centres on dysfunction in the control of ventilation. Two broad mechanisms are important: instability of ventilatory control (high loop gain) and failure of the automatic respiratory drive (reduced central respiratory output), which may be manifest as defective brainstem respiratory rhythm generation or impaired chemoreception.

Ventilatory control can be conceptualised as a negative feedback loop where changes in arterial carbon dioxide tension (PaCO2) and oxygen tension (PaO2) alter chemoreceptor activity, which in turn modulates ventilatory drive to maintain homeostasis. Loop gain is a dimensionless quantity that describes the propensity of the system to oscillate: a high loop gain implies that a small perturbation in ventilation causes an exaggerated corrective response, leading to periodic overshoots and undershoots (hyperventilation followed by hypocapnia-induced central apnoea). Patients with heart failure, stroke, or advanced age often have increased chemosensitivity (high controller gain) and prolonged circulation time (increasing feedback delay), both of which raise loop gain and predispose to Cheyne–Stokes respiration (CSR) and other periodic breathing patterns.

Increases in sensitivity of central and peripheral chemoreceptors to PaCO2 and PaO2 enhance ventilatory responses to minor changes, causing greater alternation between hyperpnea and central apnoea. For example, pulmonary congestion and neurohumoral activation in heart failure augment chemoreflex sensitivity. Prolonged circulatory time (reduced cardiac output) increases the delay between a ventilatory change and the corresponding change in sensed arterial blood gases, promoting instability because corrective ventilatory response arrives out of phase with the oscillation.

Failure of automatic drive refers to diminished baseline respiratory output from the brainstem respiratory network. During wakefulness, voluntary cortical inputs and behavioural drive can sustain ventilation even when automatic drive is impaired. During sleep—particularly non-rapid eye movement (NREM) sleep when behavioural drive wanes—loss of automatic output leads to apnoea or hypoventilation. Causes include structural lesions of the medulla or pons (ischaemia, haemorrhage, demyelination, tumour), degenerative diseases (e.g., multiple system atrophy, Parkinson-plus syndromes), metabolic suppression (severe hypothyroidism, drugs such as opioids or sedatives), and failure of chemoreceptor afferents. In congenital central hypoventilation syndrome (Ondine’s curse), genetic defects in PHOX2B alter development of autonomic and chemoreceptive neurons, producing severe loss of automatic respiratory drive that worsens during sleep.

Central hypoventilation and apnoea occur when the PaCO2 falls below the apnoeic threshold during sleep—i.e., the PaCO2 at which respiratory motor output ceases. Healthy individuals maintain PaCO2 in a range that lies above this threshold; however, in the setting of unstable ventilatory control, hyperventilatory overshoots can reduce PaCO2 below threshold, precipitating central apnoea until CO2 accumulates and drive resumes. Conversely, in true failure of automatic drive, baseline ventilatory output may be insufficient to maintain adequate alveolar ventilation, producing sustained hypoventilation with hypercapnia and hypoxemia without periodic hyperpnea.

Clinically, these mechanisms explain why CSA may present as periodic breathing (oscillatory pattern due to high loop gain) or as sustained hypoventilation (reduced automatic drive). The therapeutic implications are different: if CSA arises from high loop gain, interventions that stabilise ventilatory control—such as supplemental oxygen to blunt peripheral chemoreceptor sensitivity, carbonic anhydrase inhibitors (acetazolamide) to induce metabolic acidosis and raise baseline ventilation, or adaptive servo-ventilation to smooth ventilation—are useful. If CSA arises from structural failure of the respiratory centres, results are less predictable and may require ventilatory support that ensures adequate minute ventilation.

# Cheyne–Stokes respiration and other central patterns

Cheyne–Stokes respiration (CSR) is a classic form of central periodic breathing characterised by cyclic crescendo–decrescendo changes in tidal volume separated by central apnoeas or hypopnoeas. The cycle consists of a gradual increase in ventilation (crescendo), a peak of hyperventilation, a gradual decrease (decrescendo), and a central apnoeic or hypopnoeic phase. Cycle length is typically 30 seconds to 2 minutes, often around 45–90 seconds. CSR is strongly associated with congestive heart failure (CHF) with reduced ejection fraction, although it can occur in stroke, brainstem lesions, and high-altitude periodic breathing.

Mechanistically, CSR reflects instability in ventilatory control. Heart failure increases chemoreceptor sensitivity and prolongs circulation time; together these increase loop gain and feedback delay, facilitating large amplitude oscillations. During the hyperventilation phase PaCO2 falls below the apnoeic threshold, leading to central cessation of effort until PaCO2 rises sufficiently. The phenomenon is self-perpetuating in the context of underlying cardiopulmonary and chemoreflex abnormalities.

Other central breathing patterns include:

- Periodic breathing without full apnoea: rhythmic oscillations in tidal volume during sleep that may not reach criteria for apnoea but still cause fragmentation and gas exchange abnormalities.

- Primary central apnoea: isolated central apnoeas occurring in the absence of heart failure, medication effects, or structural brain disease; seen transiently at high altitude or during opioid withdrawal.

- Opioid-induced central apnoea and hypoventilation: opioids depress central respiratory drive and alter chemoreflex sensitivity, causing irregular breathing, central apnoeas, and sustained hypoventilation. Patterns may lack the regular oscillation seen with CSR.

- Complex sleep apnoea: arises when obstructive events are present and treated with positive airway pressure, unmasking or inducing central apnoeas—thought to reflect reduction in PaCO2 below apnoeic threshold upon correction of obstructive hypoventilation and reduction of arousals.

- Ataxic or cluster breathing: irregular, unpredictable apnoeas and varying tidal volumes with increasing severity indicating more severe brainstem dysfunction, often seen with structural lesions of the medulla.

Polysomnography demonstrates these patterns with concurrent assessment of thoracoabdominal effort. CSR is identified by the regular crescendo–decrescendo tidal volume pattern and cyclic central apnoeas. Recognising the pattern is clinically important because CSR in heart failure carries prognostic significance and may alter treatment choices.

# Impact of central apnoea on gas exchange, sleep architecture and cardiovascular risk

Central apnoea and hypoventilation have immediate effects on gas exchange. Central apnoeas produce transient reductions in alveolar ventilation, causing drops in oxygen saturation (SpO2) and rises in PaCO2 during the apnoeic interval. In periodic breathing, the alternating hyperventilation produces large swings in PaCO2 and oxygenation, with cumulative hypoxemic burden over the sleep period. Sustained central hypoventilation produces persistent hypercapnia and chronic hypoxemia, which may lead to compensatory changes in hemoglobin and pulmonary vasculature over time.

Sleep architecture is disrupted by central events via arousals triggered by chemoreflexes, hypoxemia, or intrinsic respiratory pattern termination. Frequent apnoeas and hypopneas fragment sleep, reduce slow-wave and REM sleep, and result in daytime sleepiness, impaired cognition, and reduced quality of life. Because central events can be associated with fewer large inspiratory efforts than obstructive events, they may cause less snoring but still provoke arousals via other mechanisms. Hypoventilation that predominates during REM—or in severe central hypoventilation disorders—can disproportionately impact sleep-stage-specific gas exchange, leading to REM-related nocturnal hypoventilation and morning headaches, somnolence, and cognitive dysfunction.

Cardiovascular consequences of central apnoea are complex and may be as significant as those from obstructive events. Recurrent hypoxemia, cyclic sympathetic activation on termination of apnoeas, and oscillations in intrathoracic pressure all influence autonomic balance. In CSR associated with heart failure, cyclical ventilatory changes produce oscillating sympathetic drive and may worsen arrhythmogenic risk and myocardial ischemia. Chronic intermittent hypoxia and reoxygenation promote oxidative stress, systemic inflammation, endothelial dysfunction, and atherosclerosis. Observationally, CSR and CSA in heart failure are associated with worse prognosis, heart failure progression, and increased mortality. The degree of prognostic impact depends on the underlying aetiology and severity; in heart failure patients with left ventricular dysfunction, the presence of CSR is an adverse prognostic sign.

Interventions that reduce apnoeic burden can improve sleep quality and may ameliorate some cardiovascular parameters. However, therapeutic trials have shown mixed effects on hard cardiovascular outcomes, and in some settings (e.g., adaptive servo-ventilation in certain heart failure populations with reduced ejection fraction), caution is required due to potential harm. Therefore, understanding the underlying pathophysiology and comorbid conditions is essential before instituting therapies aimed at CSA.

# Causes of acquired central apnoea including structural brainstem lesions

Acquired central apnoea can result from a broad array of conditions that either destabilise ventilatory control or impair the brainstem respiratory network directly.

Cardiac causes: Heart failure, particularly with reduced ejection fraction and prolonged circulation time, is strongly associated with CSR and central apnoea. Myocardial ischaemia and arrhythmias that reduce cardiac output can similarly predispose to periodic breathing.

Cerebrovascular and structural brain lesions: Ischaemic stroke (particularly brainstem or bilateral cortical strokes), intracerebral haemorrhage, demyelinating diseases (multiple sclerosis), tumours, and compressive lesions can damage respiratory centres in the medulla and pons. Site-specific lesions may affect the dorsal respiratory group (nucleus tractus solitarius), ventral respiratory column, pre-Bötzinger complex, and other components of respiratory rhythmogenesis or the afferent chemoreceptor pathways. Such structural insults can cause hypoventilation, ataxic breathing, or central apnoea depending on extent and localization.

Degenerative neurological diseases: Multiple system atrophy (MSA), amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS), and certain Parkinson-plus syndromes can cause progressive loss of automatic respiratory drive and upper airway control. In MSA, autonomic dysfunction and degeneration of pontomedullary respiratory centres lead to nocturnal stridor, central apnoea, and sleep-related hypoventilation.

Medication and substance effects: Opioids and benzodiazepines directly depress central respiratory drive and reduce chemosensitivity to CO2 and hypoxia. Chronic opioid therapy, especially in high doses or when combined with other sedatives, produces central apnoeas and sustained hypoventilation. Sedative-hypnotics, alcohol, and some anticonvulsants can exacerbate central hypoventilation.

Metabolic and endocrine disorders: Severe hypothyroidism can depress metabolic rate and ventilatory drive, producing hypoventilation. Central nervous system infections, sepsis with encephalopathy, and acute metabolic derangements can also transiently impair respiratory drive.

High-altitude periodic breathing: Reduced ambient oxygen at altitude increases chemoreflex sensitivity and causes periodic breathing with central apnoeas in healthy individuals; this is reversible with acclimatisation.

Postoperative and opioid-related conditions: After surgery, residual opioid analgesia, sedatives, and sleep deprivation predispose to central apnoea. Sleep-related hypoventilation may be unmasked in the postoperative period.

Congenital central hypoventilation syndrome: A rare genetic disorder (PHOX2B mutations) causes failure of automatic respiratory control, typically manifesting in infancy but with lifelong implications.

In the setting of structural brainstem lesions, the clinical presentation can include ataxic breathing (irregular amplitude and timing), cluster breathing, or persistent central apnoea. Lesions affecting the pre-Bötzinger complex tend to produce severe central hypoventilation; lesions in the nucleus tractus solitarius may disrupt chemoreceptor afferents. Identifying structural causes requires neuroimaging (MRI brainstem-focused) and correlation with other neurological signs.

# Clinical features that differentiate central from obstructive events

Differentiating central from obstructive events begins with clinical history and physical examination, but definitive differentiation requires polysomnography with respiratory effort monitoring. Clinically, OSA is strongly associated with loud snoring, witnessed apnoeas with gasping or choking, obesity, and anatomical risk factors for upper airway collapse (short neck, large tongue, retrognathia). CSA is more likely when there is minimal snoring, episodes occur in the context of heart failure, stroke, opioid use, or known brainstem disease, or when apnoeas are predominantly central on prior testing.

Key symptomatic distinctions: OSA often presents with excessive daytime sleepiness, morning headaches, and impaired concentration, though CSA can cause similar non-specific daytime symptoms. Orthopnea and paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea may point toward heart failure with possible CSR. A history of opioid use, neuromuscular disease, or prior brainstem insult raises suspicion for CSA or hypoventilation. In CSA due to hypoventilation, complaints of morning headache, daytime somnolence, and cognitive slowing are common; in CSR, breathlessness and symptoms of heart failure predominate.

On physical exam, features that support OSA include obesity (BMI > 30 kg/m^2), large neck circumference (> 40 cm in men is a commonly used threshold), crowded oropharynx (Mallampati score), and nasal obstruction. Neurological signs—cranial nerve deficits, ataxia, limb weakness, autonomic dysfunction—suggest central causes and warrant neurology referral and neuroimaging.

Polysomnographic and respiratory monitoring features: Central apnoeas are identified when airflow cessation is accompanied by absence of thoracoabdominal effort throughout the event. Obstructive apnoeas show continued or increased effort against absent airflow; often there are large negative intrathoracic pressure swings (esophageal pressure more negative) preceding arousal. The presence of a crescendo–decrescendo ventilatory pattern with cyclical central apnoeas is characteristic of CSR. In sustained hypoventilation, capnography shows elevated transcutaneous or end-tidal CO2 that remains high throughout sleep. Overnight oximetry alone cannot reliably distinguish central from obstructive events, but the pattern of oscillating desaturations without snoring may hint at central periodic breathing.

Ancillary tests and clinical decision points: When CSA is suspected, assess for contributing causes—obtain a focused cardiac evaluation (echocardiography to assess ejection fraction and exclude significant heart failure), review medications (opioids, sedatives), and consider neuroimaging if focal neurological signs or history suggests structural brainstem disease. Measure arterial blood gases if hypoventilation is suspected; chronic hypercapnia (PaCO2 > 45 mmHg) during wakefulness indicates chronic hypoventilation. Overnight monitoring of CO2 (transcutaneous or end-tidal) during PSG is increasingly recommended to detect hypoventilation; target normal awake PaCO2 is approximately 35–45 mmHg.

Clinical decision points include: if CSA occurs in heart failure with reduced ejection fraction, optimise guideline-directed medical therapy and consider nocturnal oxygen or positive airway pressure after evaluation; in opioid-induced CSA, reduce or eliminate opioid dose where feasible and consider respiratory stimulant therapies; in suspected brainstem lesion, urgent neuroimaging is indicated; and in sustained hypoventilation with daytime hypercapnia, noninvasive ventilation that guarantees adequate minute ventilation (e.g., bilevel ventilation or volume-assured modes) is generally required.

In summary, the differentiation hinges on the presence or absence of respiratory effort during events (best documented on PSG), clinical context (cardiac disease, neurological disease, opioids, obesity), and objective measurement of gas exchange including overnight CO2 monitoring. Accurate classification guides therapy and prognostication.

Conclusion

Central sleep apnoea and related hypoventilation syndromes represent a mechanistically distinct group of sleep-disordered breathing disorders driven by ventilatory control instability or failure of automatic brainstem respiratory drive. Recognising the characteristic patterns—Cheyne–Stokes respiration, periodic breathing, sustained hypoventilation—and linking them to underlying causes (heart failure, structural brainstem lesions, medications, degenerative disease) is essential for targeted management. Polysomnography with respiratory effort and CO2 monitoring, coupled with clinical evaluation for cardiac and neurological comorbidities, frames diagnostic and therapeutic decisions. Understanding the pathophysiologic basis informs appropriate interventions and clarifies why therapies effective for obstructive disease may be inadequate or contraindicated in central disorders.

Introduction

Central alveolar hypoventilation syndromes are a group of disorders characterized by impaired automatic control of ventilation such that alveolar ventilation is insufficient to maintain normal arterial carbon dioxide tension (PaCO2) and oxygenation, particularly during sleep. These syndromes may be congenital, with a genetic basis and lifelong implications, or acquired as a consequence of focal or diffuse injury to brainstem respiratory centers. Clinically they present a distinct pattern: hypoventilation that is most conspicuous during sleep, an attenuated ventilatory response to hypercapnia and hypoxia, and often a striking absence of dyspnea despite significant hypoxemia or hypercapnia. Understanding the underlying developmental and physiologic mechanisms, as well as the diagnostic approach and implications for long-term care, is essential for clinicians who encounter affected patients.

# Congenital central hypoventilation syndrome (CCHS): PHOX2B genetics and phenotype

Congenital central hypoventilation syndrome (CCHS) is the prototype congenital disorder of automatic ventilation. It is a rare neurocristopathy that manifests as impaired autonomic control of breathing and a spectrum of other autonomic nervous system abnormalities. The majority of CCHS cases are due to mutations in the PHOX2B gene, which encodes a transcription factor critical for the development of autonomic neurons derived from neural crest cells and certain brainstem nuclei involved in respiratory control.

Genetics. PHOX2B mutations fall into two broad categories. The most common are polyalanine repeat expansion mutations (PARMs), in which an in-frame expansion of a normally 20-alanine repeat tract occurs. Normal PHOX2B alleles contain 20 alanines (designated 20/20). Pathogenic PARMs most often expand this tract to 24–33 alanines (e.g., 20/25 through 20/33). Larger expansions tend to be correlated with more severe phenotypes. The less common class of mutations are non-polyalanine repeat mutations (NPARMs), including frameshift, missense, nonsense, and other in-frame deletions or insertions. NPARMs are disproportionately associated with severe phenotypes and with associated enteric and neural-crest tumor manifestations.

Phenotype and genotype-phenotype correlations. CCHS classically presents in the neonatal period with hypoventilation, usually most prominent during sleep and often necessitating ventilatory support from birth. The breathing abnormality arises from defective automatic (brainstem-mediated) control of respiration, while voluntary control may be relatively preserved. Associations include Hirschsprung disease (aganglionic megacolon) and tumors of neural crest origin, most notably neuroblastoma, ganglioneuroma, and ganglioneuroblastoma. The risk of these associated conditions is higher with certain genotypes, especially NPARMs and larger PARM expansions.

Genotype correlates with clinical severity: individuals with short PARM expansions (e.g., 20/25-20/26) may have milder phenotypes that require nocturnal ventilatory support but allow daytime spontaneous breathing, whereas those with larger expansions or NPARMs frequently require continuous ventilatory assistance and have a higher likelihood of associated Hirschsprung disease and tumor risk. Autonomic dysfunction beyond respiratory control is common and may include impaired heart rate variability, resting or exercise-related bradycardia or asystole, diminished pupillary responses, temperature dysregulation, impaired gag and swallow, and gastrointestinal dysmotility.

Pathobiology. PHOX2B plays a crucial role in the embryologic development of autonomic neurons, including those forming brainstem respiratory networks such as chemosensitive neurons in the retrotrapezoid nucleus (RTN), which are involved in central chemoreception for CO2/pH. Disruption of PHOX2B-dependent pathways results in abnormal development and function of the circuitry that generates automatic breathing and integrates chemosensory input—hence the characteristic blunted ventilatory responses and sleep-predominant hypoventilation.

# Clinical presentation across the lifespan: sleep-predominant hypoventilation, blunted chemosensitivity

Pattern of presentation. The unifying clinical feature across congenital and many acquired central hypoventilation syndromes is that impaired ventilation is most evident when wake-related voluntary control is reduced—during non-rapid eye movement (NREM) sleep and even more so during rapid eye movement (REM) sleep. Newborns with CCHS typically present with apneas, cyanosis, and hypercapnia observed on blood gases, often with minimal respiratory distress or apparent dyspnea. Older children and adults may present later with daytime somnolence, morning headaches, poor school or work performance, or newly noted sleep-disordered breathing. Some individuals with milder genotypes are diagnosed only when stressors (anesthesia, sedatives, or intercurrent illness) unmask hypoventilation.

Blunted chemosensitivity. A hallmark physiologic abnormality is reduced responsiveness of ventilatory drive to rises in arterial carbon dioxide (hypercapnia) and to hypoxemia. Chemoreception is mediated centrally by pH-sensitive neurons (central chemoreceptors) in the ventrolateral medulla (including RTN) and peripherally by carotid bodies responding to hypoxia. In CCHS, central chemoreflexes are especially impaired, leading to a markedly attenuated increase in minute ventilation for a given rise in PaCO2. Peripheral chemoreceptor responses may be variably affected, contributing to poor ventilatory response to hypoxia as well. Clinically, this means patients may tolerate very high PaCO2 (e.g., >60 mmHg) and low PaO2 without dyspnea or compensatory increases in ventilation; conversely they may be prone to dangerous hypercapnia during sleep, sedation, or when respiratory load increases.

Life-span considerations. The expression and consequences of hypoventilation evolve across the lifespan. In neonates, primary concerns are survival through the immediate perinatal period and establishing reliable ventilatory support. During infancy and childhood, growth, neurodevelopment, and risk of associated autonomic abnormalities are central issues. Adolescents and adults face transitions in care, evolving autonomy, potential refusal of therapy, and special considerations for pregnancy (in women), anesthesia, and risk during intercurrent illnesses. In acquired forms, presentation depends on lesion location and acuity: abrupt brainstem insults may cause acute, severe hypoventilation requiring urgent support; subacute or progressive lesions may produce gradual onset sleep-predominant hypoventilation and more limited daytime involvement.

# Acquired central hypoventilation: mechanisms related to brainstem compression, infarct, demyelination

Anatomical and physiological substrates. Automatic ventilation is generated by interconnected networks in the brainstem, including the pre-Bötzinger complex (rhythm generation), the ventral respiratory column, the nucleus tractus solitarius (integration of sensory input), and the retrotrapezoid nucleus/parafacial region (central chemoreception). Injury to these structures or to pathways that carry chemosensory, autonomic, or cortical modulatory inputs can produce hypoventilation that mimics congenital forms.

Mechanisms of acquired central hypoventilation. Several pathophysiologic mechanisms result in acquired central hypoventilation:

- Focal brainstem infarction or hemorrhage: Ischemic injury in the territory of the vertebrobasilar system—particularly the ventrolateral medulla, pontine tegmentum, or other structures housing respiratory rhythm or chemosensitive neurons—can acutely abolish automatic breathing. Clinical severity depends on lesion size and laterality; bilateral lesions of key regions are most likely to produce severe, persistent hypoventilation.

- Mass effect/compression: Tumors (primary brainstem gliomas, posterior fossa metastases), Chiari I malformation with tonsillar herniation, or expanding posterior fossa lesions can compress medullary respiratory centers. Compression may impair neuronal function before frank destruction, producing potentially reversible hypoventilation if decompression is performed timely.

- Demyelinating disease and inflammatory disorders: Multiple sclerosis, acute disseminated encephalomyelitis (ADEM), brainstem encephalitis, and other inflammatory processes can damage brainstem circuits intermittently or progressively. Lesions that interrupt central chemoreceptor regions or their afferent/efferent connections produce impaired automatic ventilation. The clinical course may be relapsing-remitting or progressive, with variable recovery dependent on remyelination and inflammation control.

- Traumatic brain injury: Shearing or focal injury to lower brainstem structures may interrupt respiratory networks or autonomic control. In prolonged coma, loss of automatic breath can occur, while focal brainstem injury in an otherwise conscious patient may present with sleep-predominant hypoventilation.

- Neurodegenerative disorders: Certain neurodegenerative diseases that affect brainstem autonomic nuclei (e.g., multiple system atrophy, some forms of synucleinopathies) can cause progressive impairment of automatic ventilation.

Pathophysiological consequences. Regardless of cause, the net effect is reduced automatic drive, blunted CO2 and O2 chemoreceptor responses, and impaired reflexive adjustments of ventilation to metabolic demands. The severity and reversibility vary: in compressive lesions there is potential for recovery after relief; in infarction or destructive processes recovery is limited and longer-term ventilatory support may be necessary.

# Diagnostic approach: polysomnography with CO2 monitoring, daytime ABGs and hypercapnic challenge

Clinical suspicion and initial evaluation. Diagnosis begins with high clinical suspicion in any patient with disproportionate sleep-related hypoventilation, unexplained hypercapnia, diminished respiratory symptoms despite hypoxemia, or relevant neurologic disease. A careful history should document timing (sleep vs awake), nocturnal symptoms (snoring, witnessed apneas, cyanosis), daytime sequelae (headache, somnolence), family history of CCHS, and exposure to sedatives. Physical exam should focus on cranial nerve/brainstem signs, autonomic features, and evidence of associated conditions (e.g., bowel dysfunction that might suggest Hirschsprung disease).

Polysomnography with CO2 monitoring. Overnight polysomnography (PSG) with simultaneous CO2 monitoring is central to diagnosis. Standard PSG records EEG, electro-oculography, electromyography, respiratory effort, airflow, oximetry, and can be supplemented by sensor(s) for end-tidal CO2 (PetCO2) and/or transcutaneous CO2 (PtcCO2). In central hypoventilation syndromes, PSG typically demonstrates sustained hypoventilation with reduced tidal volumes and minute ventilation during sleep, intermittent or sustained elevations in CO2, and oxygen desaturation without obstructive respiratory events (sleep apnea from central rather than obstructive origin). REM sleep may be disproportionately affected. Continuous transcutaneous CO2 monitoring is often preferred because PetCO2 can be unreliable when tidal volumes are low or there is dead space ventilation; PtcCO2 correlates reasonably with PaCO2 when calibrated and interpreted correctly.

Diagnostic thresholds. Published definitions vary by age, but useful practical thresholds include:
- Normal arterial PaCO2: 35–45 mmHg.
- Abnormal sleep hypoventilation (adult criteria commonly used): a rise in PaCO2 (or surrogate) during sleep to >45 mmHg or an increase of ≥10 mmHg above awake supine baseline; some criteria use a cutoff of PaCO2 >50 mmHg for ≥10 minutes or representing sustained hypercapnia. In pediatrics, the American Academy of Sleep Medicine suggests that an increase in PtcCO2 to >50 mmHg for >25% of total sleep time is diagnostic of sleep-related hypoventilation (criteria vary with age and must be interpreted in context).
- Daytime hypercapnia: PaCO2 >45–50 mmHg while awake and unstressed indicates hypoventilation that extends into wakefulness and often signifies more severe disease.

Daytime arterial blood gases and biochemical assessment. A baseline arterial blood gas (ABG) obtained while the patient is awake and breathing room air is important. Persistent daytime hypercapnia (PaCO2 >45–50 mmHg) indicates severe or unremitting hypoventilation and may mandate continuous ventilatory support. Measurement of bicarbonate and base excess helps identify chronicity; chronic hypercapnia often produces metabolic compensation with elevated serum bicarbonate. Normal arterial oxygen tension (PaO2) is roughly 75–100 mmHg at sea level; lower values in the setting of hypercapnia indicate alveolar hypoventilation contributing to hypoxemia.

Chemoreflex testing and hypercapnic challenge. Formal assessment of ventilatory responsiveness can be performed using controlled hypercapnic or hypoxic challenges. Examples include rebreathing tests (Read rebreathing) or controlled CO2 inhalation (e.g., 5% CO2) while measuring ventilation. The hypercapnic ventilatory response is defined as the slope of minute ventilation versus PaCO2 (or end-tidal CO2) and is markedly reduced in central hypoventilation syndromes. These studies must be done in monitored settings because patients with severe blunting of response may develop profound hypercapnia; they are most useful to characterize physiology, guide prognosis, and distinguish primary central hypoventilation from other causes of hypoventilation such as neuromuscular weakness or severe chest wall disease.

Neuroimaging and genetic testing. For suspected acquired causes, high-resolution MRI of the brainstem (brainstem and posterior fossa) is indicated to identify ischemia, demyelination, tumor, Chiari malformation, or compression. For suspected congenital cases or when family history/clinical features suggest CCHS, genetic testing for PHOX2B mutations is essential. Genotyping clarifies prognosis and guides screening for associated conditions (Hirschsprung disease, neural crest tumors). Additional evaluations include autonomic testing (heart rate variability, tilt-table testing), gastrointestinal evaluation for Hirschsprung disease (contrast studies, anorectal manometry), and tumor surveillance imaging and catecholamine metabolite assessment when indicated.

Differential diagnosis. It is critical to exclude other causes of hypoventilation such as obstructive sleep apnea, primary neuromuscular disorders (e.g., myasthenia gravis, muscular dystrophies), severe chest wall deformities, obesity hypoventilation syndrome, sedative or opioid intoxication, and chronic pulmonary disease. PSG with CO2 monitoring helps distinguish central hypoventilation from obstructive sleep apnea and quantifies the severity of hypoventilation.

# Prognosis and long-term ventilatory needs

Natural history and prognostic determinants. Prognosis depends on etiology, lesion extent, and in congenital cases, PHOX2B genotype. In CCHS, many patients require life-long ventilatory support, often starting with positive-pressure mechanical ventilation via tracheostomy in infancy. With age, some patients transition to noninvasive ventilation (bi-level positive airway pressure) for nocturnal use if their bulbar function is adequate and risk of aspiration is low. Diaphragm pacing is an option for selected patients with intact phrenic nerves and appropriate surgical candidacy; it can reduce dependence on ventilators and tracheostomy in some. The presence of daytime hypercapnia generally indicates a need for continuous (24-hour) ventilatory support. Individuals with milder genotypes may only require nocturnal ventilatory support and can maintain spontaneous daytime ventilation.

In acquired forms, prognosis is highly variable. Hypoventilation due to compressive lesions may improve after surgical decompression. Ischemic or destructive lesions often result in permanent deficits, though partial recovery can occur over months depending on neuronal plasticity and rehabilitation. The potential for recovery should be assessed over time, with serial respiratory and neurologic evaluation. In demyelinating and inflammatory disorders, disease-modifying therapy and immunosuppression can lead to improvement in some cases.

Long-term management considerations. Long-term ventilatory needs require individualized planning. For pediatric patients, growth, speech, swallowing, and neurodevelopmental support are priorities. Regular follow-up should include polysomnography with CO2 monitoring after changes in therapy, assessment of growth and development, and surveillance for associated conditions in CCHS (enteric dysfunction, tumors). For adults, monitoring includes ongoing evaluation of nocturnal ventilation adequacy, daytime gas exchange, and management of comorbidities. Transition planning from pediatric to adult care is important in congenital cases.

Device selection and monitoring. Choice of ventilatory support (invasive via tracheostomy, noninvasive positive-pressure ventilation, or diaphragm pacing) depends on severity, bulbar function, airway protection, and lifestyle goals. Home monitoring with oximetry and CO2 sensors, caregiver training, and emergency planning are essential. Regular reassessment of ventilator settings is required because growth, illness, or changes in sleep architecture can alter ventilatory requirements.

# Complications: hypoxaemia without dyspnoea, neurocognitive sequelae

Silent hypoxemia and lack of dyspnea. A striking clinical feature in central hypoventilation syndromes is the weak or absent subjective sense of breathlessness despite substantial hypoxemia and hypercapnia. This arises from impairment of the afferent and central integrative pathways that generate the experience of dyspnea. Clinically this is hazardous: patients (and caregivers) may not notice progressive respiratory compromise until hypoventilation is severe enough to cause daytime somnolence, cyanosis, or cardiopulmonary decompensation. Therefore, objective monitoring rather than symptom report is paramount in surveillance and management.

Neurocognitive sequelae. Recurrent or chronic nocturnal hypoxemia and hypercapnia are associated with adverse neurocognitive outcomes. In infants and children, disrupted oxygenation and sleep architecture during critical periods of brain development increase the risk of global developmental delay, deficits in attention and executive function, learning disability, and behavioral problems. In adults, chronic hypercapnic hypoventilation is associated with daytime somnolence, impaired attention, mood changes, and reduced quality of life. The magnitude and reversibility of these effects depend on severity and duration of gas exchange abnormalities, the presence of comorbid neurologic disease, and the timeliness and adequacy of ventilatory support.

Cardiopulmonary complications. Chronic hypoventilation can contribute to pulmonary hypertension through chronic hypoxemia-driven vasoconstriction and vascular remodeling, which may progress to right ventricular dysfunction and cor pulmonale if unrecognized. Arrhythmias including bradycardia and pauses are also a concern, especially in CCHS, where autonomic dysfunction may manifest as life-threatening bradyarrhythmias or asystole. Regular cardiac monitoring (ECG, Holter monitoring) is appropriate when indicated by symptoms or genotype.

Infectious and mechanical complications. Long-term mechanical ventilation, especially via tracheostomy, carries risks including airway infection, tracheal stenosis, and device-related complications. Noninvasive ventilation carries risks of mask-related skin breakdown, aerophagia, and inadequate ventilation if interface fit or adherence is poor. Diaphragm pacing has surgical risks and potential for device failure.

Multisystem autonomic dysfunction. In congenital forms, the widespread autonomic dysfunction can result in gastrointestinal dysmotility, temperature regulation problems, abnormal pupillary responses, and impaired baroreflexes, each of which contributes to morbidity and requires multidisciplinary management.

Summary and clinical pearls

Central alveolar hypoventilation syndromes unite a pathophysiologic theme: loss of automatic ventilatory drive with relative preservation of voluntary control. CCHS is most often due to PHOX2B mutations with genotype-phenotype correlations that inform prognosis and risk of associated neural crest disorders. Acquired forms arise from focal or diffuse injury to brainstem respiratory networks and are potentially reversible depending on cause. Diagnosis relies on polysomnography with CO2 monitoring, daytime ABGs, and assessments of chemosensitivity, combined with neuroimaging and genetic testing as indicated. Long-term care focuses on appropriate ventilatory support, monitoring for complications (including silent hypoxemia and neurocognitive sequelae), and multidisciplinary management of autonomic and associated systemic disorders. Early recognition and systematic follow-up are key to reducing morbidity and optimizing neurodevelopmental and cardiopulmonary outcomes.

# Introduction

Disorders of the craniocervical junction (CCJ) encompass a spectrum of developmental, degenerative, and acquired conditions that alter the relationship between the hindbrain and the foramen magnum. Chiari malformations — most commonly Chiari I malformation — and related CCJ abnormalities are primarily anatomic and hydrodynamic disorders: changes in bony anatomy and cerebellar position can disturb cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) pathways and lead to symptomatic brainstem and spinal cord dysfunction, most notably syringomyelia. Radiologic imaging is central to recognizing the anatomic features, quantifying severity, and guiding management decisions. This chapter reviews the neuroimaging principles and practical interpretation of CT and MRI for evaluation of Chiari and craniocervical junction disorders. Emphasis is placed on which MRI sequences are essential, how to perform and read cine phase-contrast CSF flow studies, the role and limits of CT at the foramen magnum, accepted measurement techniques for tonsillar descent and posterior fossa metrics, how to identify crowding and obliteration of subarachnoid spaces, the imaging appearance and measurement of syringomyelia and syringobulbia, and common sources of misinterpretation and inter-reader variability.

# MRI sequences critical for Chiari assessment: sagittal T1/T2, FLAIR, cine phase-contrast CSF flow studies

Magnetic resonance imaging provides the soft-tissue contrast and multiplanar capability essential for evaluating Chiari malformations and associated sequelae. A standard MRI protocol for suspected Chiari/CCJ pathology should include high-resolution sagittal T1- and T2-weighted sequences, axial T2 sequences through the brainstem and upper cervical cord, coronal images when bony or posterior fossa asymmetry is suspected, and fluid-sensitive sequences such as FLAIR when assessing concomitant supratentorial pathology. Each of these sequences has a specific utility.

Sagittal T1-weighted images give excellent anatomic delineation of the skull base, clival and basiocciput relationships, cerebellar tonsils, and brainstem orientation. T1 is useful for assessing contour and position of anatomical landmarks (clivus, dorsum sellae, foramen magnum) and for identifying hemorrhage or fat-containing lesions. T2-weighted sagittal images afford high sensitivity for CSF spaces and are the preferred sequence to visualize the relationship between the tonsils and subarachnoid CSF at the foramen magnum. T2 sagittal images also depict syrinx cavities as centrally located hyperintense signal within the cord and delineate the extent of syringomyelia.

Axial T2 sequences through the cervicomedullary junction and upper cervical cord are essential to assess cross-sectional cord morphology, to detect syrinx diameter and eccentricity, and to evaluate perimedullary CSF spaces ventrally and dorsally. Thin-section axial imaging (slice thickness \( \le 3 \) mm) through the foramen magnum improves detection of focal CSF flow restriction and visualization of tonsillar morphology (peg-like descent versus rounded tonsils).

FLAIR sequences are most helpful when there is concern for associated supratentorial white matter disease or to exclude other causes of symptoms, such as demyelination or small vessel ischemia, which may coexist or mimic Chiari-related symptoms. Post-contrast T1-weighted imaging is not routinely required for primary Chiari evaluation but should be obtained when alternative diagnoses (neoplasm, inflammation) are being considered.

Cine phase-contrast (PC) MRI — often referred to simply as “cine CSF flow” — assesses CSF dynamics across the foramen magnum. This technique encodes velocity into phase-contrast images and visualizes the pulsatile, biphasic CSF motion that normally occurs with the cardiac cycle: caudal flow during systole and rostral flow during diastole. Cine PC studies may be performed as qualitative cine images or as quantitative assessments yielding peak velocities and stroke volumes. Proper technique typically acquires through-plane velocity-encoded data perpendicular to the plane of CSF flow at the level of the foramen magnum or C1. Typical velocity-encoding settings (VENC) for foramen magnum studies are low, often in the range of 5–10 cm/s, to maximize sensitivity to slow CSF motion; selecting too high a VENC will decrease sensitivity, while too low a VENC risks aliasing. Careful cardiac gating (pulse oximeter or ECG) and acquisition over multiple cardiac phases (e.g., 16–32 frames per cardiac cycle) produce cine loops that show dynamic obstruction, jetting, or absence of flow. Cine PC is most informative when correlated with anatomic images: absence or marked attenuation of caudal and rostral flow at the foramen magnum supports a hydrodynamic obstruction that may contribute to syrinx formation, even when the degree of tonsillar descent is modest.

# CT imaging roles and limitations at the foramen magnum

Computed tomography (CT) is complementary to MRI in the evaluation of craniocervical junction pathology because of its superior resolution of osseous anatomy. High-resolution CT with bone algorithm reconstructions is the modality of choice for assessing congenital bony anomalies (occipitalization of C1, basilar invagination, assimilation of the atlas), traumatic fractures, atlanto-occipital instability, abnormal condylar morphology, and the size and orientation of the foramen magnum. CT can detect osteophytes, calcified ligaments, and lytic lesions that may narrow the foramen magnum or otherwise alter the osseous boundaries of the posterior fossa. Thin-slice CT with multiplanar reformats (sagittal and coronal) provides precise landmarks for surgical planning, particularly in posterior fossa decompression or posterior fixation procedures.

However, CT has significant limitations in Chiari assessment. CT provides poor soft-tissue contrast for cerebellar tonsils, brainstem parenchyma, and intramedullary cavities, making it unsuitable as a primary modality for assessing tonsillar morphology or syringomyelia. CT cannot evaluate CSF flow dynamics, which are crucial to understanding pathophysiology in many Chiari cases. Radiation exposure is another drawback, particularly in children and young adults, where serial imaging may be required. Even with thin slices, CT may miss subtle soft-tissue crowding at the ventral subarachnoid space. Therefore, CT should be used as an adjunct to MRI when bony detail is required or when MRI is contraindicated.

# Measurement techniques for tonsillar descent and posterior fossa metrics

Accurate and reproducible measurement of cerebellar tonsillar position and posterior fossa dimensions is fundamental to the radiologic diagnosis and grading of Chiari I malformation and related CCJ disorders. A number of anatomic reference lines and metrics are in common use; the interpreter must explicitly state the line or method used, because different lines yield different numeric values.

The most widely used measurement for tonsillar descent is the perpendicular distance from the inferior margin of the cerebellar tonsils to the foramen magnum, most commonly referenced to the McRae line. The McRae line is drawn between the basion and opisthion (the anterior and posterior margins of the foramen magnum); tonsillar descent is measured as the vertical distance below this line in millimeters on the midline sagittal image. Historically, cerebellar tonsils extending more than 5 mm below the foramen magnum by this measurement have been labeled Chiari I malformation in adults; however, the 5 mm threshold is arbitrary and should not be used alone to direct management — clinical correlation is essential. Tonsillar position from 0 to 5 mm below the foramen magnum may be a normal variant, particularly in adolescents and in patients with small posterior fossae. Pediatric definitions are more complex: in children, tonsils can be lower without pathology, and age-based thresholds or percentiles are sometimes applied.

Alternative reference lines include Chamberlain’s line (posterior edge of hard palate to opisthion) and McGregor’s line (posterior hard palate to caudal occiput), which are primarily used to assess basilar invagination rather than routine tonsillar descent. The tonsillar descent distance should always be reported with the reference line used and the imaging plane (midline sagittal) and should note whether the tonsillar tip is rounded, peg-shaped, or flattened, as morphology can have diagnostic and prognostic significance.

Posterior fossa morphometrics aim to quantify whether the posterior fossa is “small” or hypoplastic, which is one hypothesized cause of tonsillar herniation. Common measures include posterior fossa height (distance from the dorsum sellae to the internal occipital protuberance or opisthion), clival length (from the dorsum sellae to the foramen magnum), and posterior cranial fossa area or volume. Volumetric assessment — typically performed with dedicated post-processing and segmentation of the posterior cranial fossa — provides the most physiologically relevant measure but is more resource intensive. A simple index used in several studies is the posterior fossa crowdedness index, which is the ratio of hindbrain tissue (cerebellum and brainstem) area to posterior fossa area on midsagittal sections; increased ratios suggest crowding. Tentorial angle and basal angle may also be reported; a more acute tentorial angle can exacerbate crowding. No single posterior fossa metric has universal acceptance; rather, combined morphometrics interpreted in the clinical and radiographic context provide the most value.

When giving reported numbers, radiologists should include the measurement technique, the exact numeric result in millimeters, and any relevant normative range or institutional cutoff used. For example: “Tonsillar descent 7 mm below the McRae line on midsagittal T2-weighted image.”

# Identifying crowded foramen magnum and obliteration of subarachnoid spaces

Anatomic crowding at the foramen magnum is central to Chiari pathophysiology: reduced space around the cerebellar tonsils and at the CCJ can obstruct CSF flow between cranial and spinal compartments. Imaging evidence of crowding includes obliteration or narrowing of the dorsal and ventral subarachnoid cisterns, effacement of the prepontine or premedullary cisterns, and compression of the cisterna magna.

On sagittal and axial T2-weighted images, the subarachnoid space should appear as a continuous hyperintense rim of CSF around the brainstem and upper cervical cord. Obliteration is visualized as the loss of that hyperintense layer adjacent to the tonsils or brainstem due to soft tissue approximation to the bone or spinal cord. The ventral subarachnoid space (ventral to the brainstem) and dorsal subarachnoid space (dorsal to the tonsils) should be assessed separately; isolated ventral compression (e.g., from basilar invagination causing anterior brainstem impingement) can produce symptoms and CSF disturbance even when dorsal CSF spaces appear relatively preserved.

Axial imaging at the level of the foramen magnum is particularly useful to describe circumferential crowding and to identify focal jetting or flow channels between tonsil and bone. The presence of arachnoid adhesions (when visible) or scarring in postoperative patients can be inferred from fixed focal obliteration. While MRI delineates soft tissue and CSF, cine PC studies add dynamic information: markedly reduced or absent bidirectional pulsatile CSF flow at the foramen magnum indicates functional obstruction even when residual static CSF is visible on T2 images.

Interpretation must account for physiologic variables: head position and neck flexion/extension alter tonsillar position and CSF space geometry; therefore, if clinical suspicion is high but imaging looks equivocal, consider repeat imaging with neutral head position or dynamic maneuvers in specialized centers. Also, artifacts from flow voids or partial-volume averaging can mimic or obscure small CSF spaces, reinforcing the need for thin-section imaging and consistent protocoling.

# Detection and assessment of syringomyelia and syringobulbia

Syringomyelia — a fluid-filled cavity within the spinal cord — is one of the most important complications associated with Chiari malformations. Syringobulbia is the extension of such cavities into the brainstem. MRI is the gold standard for detection, characterization, and longitudinal monitoring of syrinx cavities.

On T2-weighted images, syrinx cavities appear as well-defined, centrally located hyperintense regions within the spinal cord parenchyma. They may be simple tubular cavities or more complex, septated, or eccentric shapes. T1-weighted images usually show corresponding hypointensity. Contrast enhancement of the cavity walls is not typical and, if present, should prompt consideration of alternative diagnoses such as neoplasm, inflammatory conditions, or post-surgical change.

When reporting a syrinx, the following descriptive elements are clinically important: location (vertebral level span), maximal diameter, relationship to the cord (central versus eccentric), and associated T2 or T1 signal changes in the adjacent cord that may reflect myelomalacia. Quantitative assessment should include the maximal transverse diameter in millimeters and the syrinx-to-cord ratio (maximum syrinx diameter divided by the maximal cord diameter at the same level), because this ratio has prognostic relevance: a syrinx occupying a larger proportion of the cord cross-section is more likely to be symptomatic or to progress. Length is usually reported in vertebral segments (e.g., extending from C2 to T4) rather than centimeters. Longitudinal extension into the medulla indicates syringobulbia and requires careful assessment of lower cranial nerve nuclei and the floor of the fourth ventricle.

Differential diagnoses include hydromyelia (dilatation of the central canal continuous with the fourth ventricle, often more common in infants), intrinsic intramedullary neoplasm, post-traumatic cysts, and focal gliosis. In cases where the syrinx is small or equivocal, high-resolution axial sequences are helpful to distinguish an eccentrically located syrinx from an extrinsic compressive lesion. Additionally, if there is suspected communication between ventricular CSF and the syrinx, sequences that visualize the fourth ventricle and foramen of Magendie (sagittal T2) and cine flow imaging can be instructive.

Monitoring strategy is determined by symptoms and imaging: asymptomatic small syrinxes may be observed with interval MRI, while enlarging syringes, progressive neurological signs, or persistent CSF flow obstruction on cine PC studies often prompt surgical consideration. When serial imaging is performed, consistent technique (slice thickness, plane, and sequence type) is essential to allow meaningful comparison of syrinx size over time.

# Common radiologic pitfalls and inter-reader variability

Interpreting neuroimaging for Chiari and CCJ disorders is subject to multiple pitfalls that can lead to overdiagnosis or underestimation of disease. Awareness of these limitations is essential for clinicians relying on imaging to make management decisions.

One major pitfall is rigid reliance on a numerical tonsillar descent threshold (commonly >5 mm) without clinical correlation. Tonsillar position varies with age, body habitus, and head position, and mild descent (1–5 mm) may be a normal variant. Conversely, symptomatic patients occasionally have minimal tonsillar descent but pronounced CSF flow obstruction due to crowding or arachnoid adhesions. Therefore, imaging reports should integrate anatomy, CSF flow studies, posterior fossa metrics, and clinical context.

Technical factors introduce variability. Slice thickness and plane positioning affect measured tonsillar descent: thicker slices and off-midline sagittal cuts can underestimate the true extent. Head flexion or extension during the examination can increase or decrease observed tonsillar excursion. Partial-volume effects may create the appearance of a peg-like tonsil when in fact the tonsil is rounded. Motion artifact, particularly in uncooperative patients or in long cine sequences, degrades image quality and complicates flow measurements.

Cine phase-contrast imaging is susceptible to specific technical pitfalls: inappropriate velocity encoding (VENC) leads to underestimation of flow (too high) or aliasing (too low).
