Cardiac gating errors or irregular heart rhythms produce inconsistent phase images. Interpretation errors occur when relying solely on static measurements rather than viewing the dynamic cine loop; focal jets may be brief and localized, requiring cine playback to appreciate. Postoperative patients present another challenge: persistent flow on cine does not always correlate with symptomatic improvement, and scar tissue may produce focal flow abnormalities that are hard to distinguish from residual or recurrent obstruction.

Inter-reader variability is well documented in interpretation of Chiari-related imaging. Studies have shown significant variability in measurement of tonsillar descent, grading of crowding, and interpretation of cine flow. Sources of variability include choice of reference line (McRae vs Chamberlain), measurement method (perpendicular distance vs vertical projection), the observer’s experience, and institutional protocol differences. Standardization mitigates variability: always measure on the midline sagittal image, explicitly state the reference line used, use thin-section imaging, and include cine PC studies with standardized VENC and gating parameters when possible. When a second opinion will affect management, involvement of neuroradiologists with specific experience in CCJ imaging is advisable.

Finally, clinical overinterpretation of incidental findings is a hazard. Incidental low-lying tonsils discovered on imaging done for unrelated reasons should be interpreted conservatively: in asymptomatic patients, imaging findings alone rarely mandate intervention. Management decisions should be driven by symptomatology, neurological examination, and longitudinal imaging trends rather than a single arbitrary measurement.

# Conclusion

Accurate and clinically meaningful neuroimaging for Chiari malformations and craniocervical junction disorders requires attention to both high-quality anatomic sequences and dynamic assessment of CSF flow. Sagittal T1 and T2 sequences, thin-section axial imaging, fluid-sensitive sequences, and cine phase-contrast MRI together provide the necessary information to evaluate tonsillar position, posterior fossa crowding, CSF dynamics, and syringomyelia. CT is a valuable adjunct for osseous anatomy but does not replace MRI for soft-tissue and CSF evaluation. Standardized measurement techniques, clear reporting of reference lines and parameters, and awareness of technical and physiologic sources of variability reduce interpretive errors. Ultimately, integration of imaging findings with the clinical presentation and longitudinal assessment guides appropriate management and avoids both under‑ and over-treatment.

Introduction

Polysomnography (PSG) is the integrated recording of multiple physiologic signals during sleep and is the gold standard for diagnosing sleep-disordered breathing, parasomnias, movement disorders, and disorders of sleep architecture. A clinical understanding of the component signals, how they are recorded and interpreted, and the strengths and limitations of different types of sleep studies is essential for diagnosing and managing patients with suspected obstructive sleep apnoea (OSA), central sleep apnoea (CSA), hypoventilation syndromes, and complex sleep pathophysiology. This chapter reviews the physiologic basis and technical aspects of the core PSG channels, contrasts full laboratory (type I) testing with the limited device classifications (type II–III), explains contemporary scoring criteria for respiratory events including the separation of central and obstructive events, defines the apnoea–hypopnoea index (AHI) and its clinical thresholds, and describes the role of carbon dioxide monitoring and common sources of error that lead to over- or under-estimation of disease severity.

# Polysomnography components: EEG, EOG, EMG, airflow, respiratory effort, oximetry, ECG

The standard polysomnogram integrates neurophysiologic and cardiorespiratory signals to permit simultaneous assessment of sleep stages, arousals, respiratory events, oxygenation, cardiac rhythm, and limb movements. Each channel serves a specific purpose and has characteristic placement and signals that must be understood to interpret PSG correctly.

Electroencephalography (EEG)
EEG is the foundation for sleep staging. Typical clinical PSG uses multiple scalp electrodes referenced and placed according to a simplified montage (for example, C3-A2, C4-A1, O1-A2, O2-A1) to capture cortical activity. EEG allows recognition of wakefulness (low-amplitude mixed-frequency activity, eye-blink artifacts), NREM sleep stages N1–N3 (transitioning from theta to high-amplitude slow-wave delta), and REM sleep (low-voltage mixed-frequency with sawtooth waves). Sleep staging follows standardized criteria (commonly AASM) using 30-second epochs. EEG also detects cortical arousals—abrupt shifts in frequency lasting ≥3 seconds (with separate criteria in children)—which are important in scoring respiratory-related arousals and determining sleep fragmentation.

Electro-oculography (EOG)
EOG channels record horizontal and sometimes vertical eye movements. EOG distinguishes N1 (slow-rolling eye movements), REM (rapid eye movements), and wakefulness. Eye movement patterns, together with EEG and EMG, are essential to identify REM sleep and detect REM-related respiratory events (e.g., REM-predominant OSA).

Electromyography (EMG)
Chin EMG is used to identify muscle tone and to differentiate REM (muscle atonia) from NREM; reduction in chin EMG is part of REM definition. Limb EMG (usually anterior tibialis) records periodic limb movements during sleep (PLMS), which can cause arousals and influence the respiratory event assessment. EMG signals are also used to detect bruxism and note movement artifact that may simulate respiratory events.

Airflow
Airflow measurement is critical to detect apnoea and hypopnea. Nasal pressure transducers provide a sensitive surrogate for airflow and are especially better at detecting partial airflow reductions (hypopnea). Thermistors or thermocouples detect thermal fluctuations and are good at discriminating flow cessation (apnea) but less sensitive for partial flow reduction. Current practice often records both: nasal pressure for quantitative amplitude changes and a thermistor for confirmation of apneic cessation. Signal quality, sensor placement, and nasal obstruction influence the fidelity of these channels.

Respiratory effort
Respiratory effort belts around the chest and abdomen (inductance plethysmography or respiratory inductance plethysmography) record thoracoabdominal motion. The presence versus absence of respiratory effort during a cessation of airflow is the key distinction between obstructive and central events. In obstructive apnoea the belts show continued or paradoxical effort despite absent airflow; in central apnoea there is an absence of thoracoabdominal movement. Careful attention to belt placement, calibration (if used for quantitative measurement), and recognition of belt signal artifact (e.g., movement, poor contact) is necessary.

Oximetry
Pulse oximetry measures arterial oxygen saturation (SpO2). Pulse oximetry provides critical objective evidence of impairment in gas exchange associated with respiratory events. Standard oximeters sample at rates of 1 Hz or greater; averaging times and signal filtering affect the depth and timing of desaturation reported. Typical normal awake SpO2 is 95–100% at sea level; nighttime desaturation episodes tied to apnoea/hypopnea are used in scoring hypopneas (see later). Frequent technical issues—motion artifact, poor perfusion, nail polish—must be considered.

Electrocardiography (ECG)
A single-lead ECG is routinely recorded during PSG to monitor heart rhythm and to identify arrhythmias or cardiac events that may be caused or unmasked by sleep-disordered breathing. ECG also assists in identifying arousals associated with heart rate accelerations and in research contexts for heart rate variability analysis. Typical placement mirrors lead II or a modified lead to optimize R-wave detection.

Other sensors
Many PSGs include additional channels such as position sensors, snore microphones, video monitoring, and end-tidal or transcutaneous CO2 monitoring. Each can augment interpretation: body position is relevant for positional OSA, snoring helps confirm flow limitation, and video documents parasomnias.

# Differences between type I (full) and limited (type II–III) studies and their diagnostic limitations

Sleep studies are classified by the number and types of channels recorded and by whether they are performed attended in the laboratory. Understanding these classifications is important when selecting the appropriate test and when interpreting outcomes.

Type I — Full attended polysomnography
Type I studies are comprehensive attended overnight PSGs performed in a sleep laboratory with simultaneous neurophysiologic, respiratory, cardiac, and oxygenation monitoring (including EEG, EOG, EMG, airflow, respiratory effort, oximetry, and ECG). The presence of EEG allows accurate sleep staging and assessment of total sleep time (TST), arousals, and sleep architecture. This is the gold standard for diagnosing a broad range of sleep disorders, particularly when differential diagnosis requires sleep stage information (e.g., REM-related OSA, REM behavior disorder, narcolepsy suspicion, parasomnias, complex cases, or suspected hypoventilation).

Type II — Unattended full PSG (portable, comprehensive)
Type II devices record the same physiologic signals as type I but are typically unattended and performed in the home environment. They usually include EEG channels, airflow, effort, oximetry, and often ECG. Type II provides the advantage of recording sleep and wake, but because they are unattended, signal loss and artifact are more common; equipment setup errors may degrade EEG or other channels.

Type III — Limited channel sleep apnea testing
Type III devices record a minimum of four channels, typically including airflow (often nasal pressure), respiratory effort (thoracic and/or abdominal belt), oximetry, and ECG or heart rate. They do not include EEG/EOG/EMG; therefore they cannot determine sleep stages or total sleep time. They are often used for home sleep apnea testing (HSAT) when OSA is strongly suspected and comorbidities are absent. Advantages are convenience, lower cost, and greater accessibility.

Type IV — Very limited devices
Type IV devices record 1–3 channels, often just oximetry and/or airflow. Their diagnostic accuracy is limited and they are generally only used for screening or in situations with resource constraints.

Diagnostic limitations and implications
The absence of EEG in type III/IV devices has several important consequences. Without EEG you cannot reliably determine total sleep time, so event rates must be normalized to recording time (see later). This commonly results in underestimation of severity when sleep efficiency is low (i.e., wake time during recording dilutes event frequency). Conversely, devices that infer sleep from absence of movement or changes in actigraphy may misclassify quiet wakefulness as sleep and thus overestimate or underestimate events. Type III devices are accurate in patients with high pretest probability of moderate-to-severe OSA and without significant cardiopulmonary or neuromuscular disease. They are inappropriate when central sleep apnea, hypoventilation, neuromuscular weakness, or other sleep disorders are suspected—conditions that require EEG and often CO2 monitoring. Unattended studies also have higher rates of technical failure; careful quality control and patient selection are essential.

# Scoring criteria for apnoea, hypopnoea and central vs obstructive events

Scoring respiratory events uses standardized rules (commonly AASM 2012/2017 updates) that define the magnitude and duration of airflow reduction and associated physiologic consequences. While small differences exist among guideline versions, the core definitions remain: apnoea is near-complete airflow cessation; hypopnea is a partial reduction with physiologic consequence; central vs obstructive apnoea is distinguished by respiratory effort.

Apnoea
An apnoea is typically defined as a drop in airflow amplitude by at least 90% of baseline for at least $10$ seconds. The airflow source can be thermistor, nasal pressure, or a combination; most scorers require confirmation across devices and consider sensor limitations. Events are labeled as obstructive, central, or mixed depending on respiratory effort.

- Obstructive apnoea: Airflow is absent or nearly absent while thoracoabdominal effort persists and may be paradoxical. The presence of continued respiratory effort on belts distinguishes obstructive apnoea.
- Central apnoea: Airflow cessation is accompanied by a cessation of respiratory effort (flat or near-flat effort signals) for the duration of the event. Central apnoeas may be idiopathic, heart-failure-related, opioid-induced, or related to instability in ventilatory control.
- Mixed apnoea: Begins as central (no effort) and ends with renewed respiratory effort; often scored as mixed but classified by the predominant component (practice varies).

Hypopnoea
Hypopnoea definitions have evolved and commonly require both a reduction in airflow amplitude and an associated oxygen desaturation or EEG arousal. A frequently used definition is a reduction in nasal pressure or airflow amplitude by ≥30% of baseline for ≥10 s associated with either ≥3% oxygen desaturation or an arousal (this is known as the AASM "recommended" rule). Some scoring systems use a ≥4% desaturation criterion in lieu of arousal documentation. The choice of 3% versus 4% thresholds affects prevalence estimates—using a 3%/arousal rule identifies more events than a 4% desaturation-only rule.

Central vs obstructive hypopnoea
Distinguishing central from obstructive hypopnoeas is more nuanced. If respiratory effort decreases substantially or is absent during the reduced airflow, the event is considered central; if respiratory effort persists, it is considered obstructive. Criteria often require analysis of thoracoabdominal signals: a paradoxical pattern or continued effort indicates flow limitation from upper-airway obstruction. Flow-limited waveforms on nasal pressure (flattening of the inspiratory contour) can support obstructive physiology even when effort belts show preserved motion.

Associated events and rules
Events are typically scored only during sleep epochs; events during wake are excluded. Events separated by less than $2$ breaths are often considered part of a single prolonged event. Periodic breathing and Cheyne–Stokes respiration are scored when cyclic crescendo–decrescendo ventilation with central apnoeas/hypopnoeas is present over a sustained period and meet numerical thresholds.

# Apnoea–hypopnoea index (AHI) calculation and interpretation

Definition and calculation
The apnoea–hypopnoea index (AHI) quantifies sleep-disordered breathing burden by dividing the total number of apnoeas plus hypopnoeas during a sleep study by the sleep time in hours:

AHI = (Number of apnoeas + Number of hypopnoeas) / Total sleep time in hours

For type I and type II studies that include EEG, total sleep time should be used. For type III devices lacking EEG, some reporting uses total recording time (TRT) rather than TST—this must be explicitly stated because it affects interpretation (see the section on sources of error).

Clinical thresholds
Although thresholds are somewhat arbitrary and outcomes are continuous, commonly used AHI categorical cutoffs are:

- Normal: AHI < 5 events/hour
- Mild OSA: AHI 5–14.9 events/hour
- Moderate OSA: AHI 15–29.9 events/hour
- Severe OSA: AHI ≥ 30 events/hour

Clinical interpretation
The AHI guides diagnosis and management but must be interpreted in clinical context. Symptoms (excessive daytime sleepiness, impaired cognition, cardiovascular comorbidity), observed apnoea, and comorbid conditions influence management decisions. Two patients with identical AHIs may have very different clinical risks depending on oxygen desaturation severity, sleep fragmentation, REM-related predominance, positional effects, and underlying cardiopulmonary disease.

Alternative indices
Other indices include the respiratory disturbance index (RDI), which may include respiratory-effort-related arousals (RERAs) in addition to apnoeas/hypopneas, and the oxygen desaturation index (ODI), which counts desaturation events per hour (commonly ≥3% or ≥4%). RDI is useful when RERAs contribute to sleep-disordered breathing but requires EEG/arousal detection.

Limitations of AHI
AHI treats all events equivalently regardless of associated desaturation or arousal; it does not capture event duration, depth of desaturation, CO2 retention, or clinical consequences. A patient with a modest AHI but prolonged desaturations or significant hypercapnia may have greater physiologic impairment than AHI alone suggests. Thus, AHI should be integrated with oximetry, CO2 monitoring when indicated, and clinical features.

# Utility of end-tidal and transcutaneous CO2 monitoring for hypoventilation

Rationale for CO2 monitoring
Pulse oximetry reflects oxygenation but is an insensitive marker for hypoventilation in many settings: supplemental oxygen can mask hypoventilation, and oxygen desaturation can lag behind rising CO2. Monitoring of carbon dioxide partial pressure provides direct physiological assessment of alveolar ventilation and is essential for diagnosing hypoventilation syndromes (e.g., obesity hypoventilation syndrome (OHS), neuromuscular weakness, chest wall deformity, opioid-induced respiratory depression, and central hypoventilation syndromes).

Modality options: end-tidal CO2 (P_ETCO2) and transcutaneous CO2 (P_tcCO2)
End-tidal CO2 (P_ETCO2) measures the CO2 concentration at the end of exhalation, typically using mainstream or sidestream capnography. It is widely used because of its immediacy and noninvasiveness. However, P_ETCO2 reflects alveolar gas and can underestimate arterial CO2 (PaCO2) due to ventilation–perfusion mismatch, airway obstruction, or sampling issues. The typical gradient (PaCO2 – P_ETCO2) in healthy subjects is small (1–5 mmHg) but can be larger in disease.

Transcutaneous CO2 (P_tcCO2) measures CO2 diffusing through heated skin using an electrochemical sensor; it approximates PaCO2 after a calibration and equilibration period and is less influenced by breath-by-breath variation or entrainment problems. P_tcCO2 is commonly used during sleep studies when continuous CO2 monitoring is required, particularly in patients with suspected hypoventilation, neuromuscular disease, or opioid therapy.

Diagnostic thresholds for hypoventilation
Consensus and guideline statements suggest specific criteria for defining sleep-associated hypoventilation:

- Sustained hypercapnia: P_tcCO2 (or PaCO2) > 55 mmHg for ≥10 minutes during sleep is considered diagnostic of significant hypoventilation.
- Relative rise: A rise in P_tcCO2 of ≥10 mmHg above awake supine baseline to a value >50 mmHg for ≥10 minutes is also used as a criterion.

Different societies may apply slightly different numerical cutoffs; clinicians should apply the definitions used by their local laboratory and consider the clinical context. Important practical thresholds include awake PaCO2 > 45 mmHg often indicating daytime hypoventilation, whereas nocturnal elevations that meet the above criteria indicate sleep hypoventilation.

Advantages and limitations
P_ETCO2 advantages: immediate response to ventilatory changes, simple setup for many patients, good for detecting events with preserved tidal volumes. Limitations: underestimation of PaCO2 with COPD, airway obstruction, or mask leaks; sidestream delays; and dependence on nasal breathing for sampling accuracy. P_tcCO2 advantages: better correlation with PaCO2 over prolonged monitoring, less sensitive to mouth breathing or minor leaks. Limitations: site heating can cause skin irritation or burns if improperly used, sensor drift over prolonged recordings, requirement for calibration and equilibration (several minutes), and potential lag in tracking rapid changes.

Clinical applications
CO2 monitoring is essential when hypoventilation is suspected: evaluation of obesity hypoventilation syndrome (OHS), neuromuscular disease, advanced COPD with suspected hypercapnic respiratory failure, or opioid-induced respiratory depression. It should be used in the lab when treating with or titrating noninvasive ventilation and when oxygen therapy may mask hypercapnia. Interpretation requires understanding of sensor-specific biases and correlation with arterial blood gas when necessary.

# Sources of under- and over-estimation (e.g., total recording time vs total sleep time)

Estimating event frequency and severity is vulnerable to several sources of error that can lead to under- or over-estimation of disease. Recognizing these sources is essential for accurate reporting and clinical decision-making.

Total recording time (TRT) versus total sleep time (TST)
The most common and important source of discrepancy is whether the denominator used to calculate an event index is total recording time (minutes from lights-off to lights-on or from device start to stop) or total sleep time (epochs scored as sleep). Type I/II PSGs with EEG use TST, which is the physiologic correct denominator. Type III/IV studies typically lack EEG and therefore often normalize events to TRT. If a patient has poor sleep efficiency (many wake epochs) the use of TRT will dilute event frequency and underestimate AHI relative to a PSG-derived AHI using TST. Conversely, if devices or algorithms misclassify wake as sleep (e.g., based on actigraphy or low movement), event rates may be overestimated.

Sensor failure and signal artifact
Loss of key channels (e.g., nasal pressure, thermistor, effort belts, oximetry) leads to missed events or misclassification. For example, if the nasal cannula is dislodged, hypopneas that depend on nasal pressure amplitude reduction may not be detected, causing underestimation. Conversely, motion artifact on nasal pressure or belts can create spurious fluctuations scored as events, leading to overestimation. EEG lead loss may prevent scoring of arousals; hypopneas defined by arousal may therefore be missed.

Use of different airflow sensors and scoring rules
Thermistors detect flow cessation well but are less sensitive for hypopneas; nasal pressure is more sensitive for partial reductions. Studies that use only thermistors will tend to under-detect hypopneas relative to those using nasal pressure. Similarly, the choice of hypopnea scoring rule (≥3% desaturation or arousal versus ≥4% desaturation-only) has a direct impact on event counts—3%/arousal rules produce higher AHI values. Therefore, reports must document the sensor types and scoring criteria used.

Averaging time and oximeter response
Pulse oximeters have adjustable averaging times and filtering. Longer averaging blunts rapid desaturations and may underestimate nadir SpO2 and the ODI. Irregular heart rates, low perfusion, or motion can produce erroneous desaturations (overestimation) or loss of signal (underestimation). When oxygen is administered, SpO2 may remain normal despite hypoventilation; reliance on SpO2 alone in these settings is dangerous.

Body position and sleep stage distribution
Patients may spend disproportionate time in supine position or in REM sleep during a single-night study, and both positional and REM-related OSA can produce night-to-night variability. A single-night PSG may under- or over-represent habitual sleeping conditions. Home studies without positional sensors may miss positional dependency leading to underestimation of disease amenable to positional therapy.

Event duration weighting and desaturation severity
AHI counts events equally regardless of duration. A few prolonged apnoeas producing deep desaturation may be more physiologically impactful than many brief events. Exclusive reliance on AHI may therefore misrepresent risk. Measures such as T90 (percentage of sleep time with SpO2 < 90%), mean and nadir SpO2, ODI, and event duration distributions provide additional clinically relevant information.

Algorithms and auto-scoring
Automated scoring algorithms can process large datasets rapidly but vary in sensitivity and specificity. Many automated systems require expert review; overreliance on unattended auto-scoring may yield both false positives (artifacts scored as events) and false negatives (missed subtle events, RERAs). Best practice is human review and correction, especially for borderline or complex studies.

Patient factors and co-morbid conditions
Nasal obstruction, mouth breathing, obesity, COPD, heart failure, and neuromuscular disease all influence signal quality and physiology. For example, COPD causes V/Q mismatch that widens the PaCO2–P_ETCO2 gradient, reducing the accuracy of end-tidal CO2 as a surrogate for arterial hypercapnia and potentially masking hypoventilation on capnography. Supplemental oxygen can mask desaturation but not hypercapnia. Opioids can produce periodic respiration and hypoventilation that may be under-recognized on limited-channel studies.

Practical recommendations to mitigate errors
To minimize under- and over-estimation: choose the appropriate study type for the clinical question (use full PSG with CO2 monitoring when hypoventilation or complex sleep disorders are suspected); ensure careful sensor placement and technician oversight; document the sensors and scoring rules used; review for and correct artifacts; and interpret AHI in conjunction with oxygenation metrics, CO2 data when available, sleep architecture, and clinical presentation. Explicitly report whether indices are normalized to TST or TRT and include TST, TRT, sleep efficiency, ODI, mean and nadir SpO2, and total time with elevated CO2 when present.

Conclusion

An integrated understanding of PSG components, their physiologic basis, the classifications of sleep study types, standardized scoring criteria, and the role of CO2 monitoring is essential for accurate diagnosis and management of sleep-disordered breathing and hypoventilation. Recognizing the limitations and potential sources of error—particularly the difference between total recording time and total sleep time, sensor-specific biases, and patient-specific factors—permits appropriate selection of testing modalities and cautious interpretation of results in clinical practice.

# Introduction

Monitoring respiratory status at the bedside and in the laboratory is a core skill for clinicians who care for acutely ill patients, those with chronic respiratory disease, and patients receiving sedation or respiratory support. Oxygenation and ventilation are distinct physiological processes: oxygenation refers to the loading of oxygen onto hemoglobin and the oxygen content of arterial blood, whereas ventilation refers to the movement of carbon dioxide out of the body and the control of arterial carbon dioxide tension. The clinician must be able to integrate noninvasive bedside monitors (pulse oximetry, capnography, transcutaneous CO2), nocturnal studies, and arterial blood gas (ABG) results to recognize hypoxemia, hypercapnia, ventilation–perfusion mismatch, and to guide therapy. This chapter explains the principles, strengths and limitations of common monitoring modalities and provides a framework for interpreting ABGs and the alveolar–arterial gradient, with a focus on the situations in which pulse oximetry may fail to detect important ventilatory problems.

# Pulse oximetry technology, accuracy, artefacts and limitations (motion, poor perfusion, sensor type)

Pulse oximetry is a noninvasive method to estimate arterial hemoglobin oxygen saturation (SpO2). The device uses two wavelengths of light—typically red (~660 nm) and infrared (~940 nm)—transmitted through a vascular bed (finger, earlobe, forehead). The technology relies on the different absorption spectra of oxyhemoglobin and deoxyhemoglobin and on the pulsatile nature of arterial flow to segregate arterial from venous and tissue absorption. The ratio of pulsatile changes at the two wavelengths is mapped to an SpO2 value by device-specific calibration curves derived from healthy volunteers.

Accuracy and performance
Most modern pulse oximeters have accuracy characterized by an root mean square (RMS) error or ARMS, typically ±2% for saturations between 90% and 100% under good conditions. Accuracy degrades below SpO2 of 80%, where error may be considerably larger. Important performance characteristics:

- Precision tends to be better in the 90–100% range than at lower saturations.
- Many devices report only functional oxygen saturation (percentage of hemoglobin bound to oxygen) and cannot distinguish dyshemoglobinemias (carboxyhemoglobin, methemoglobin) or significant anemia.
- SpO2 is an estimate of SaO2 (arterial oxygen saturation measured from arterial blood). Discrepancies can occur because SpO2 is influenced by probe placement, perfusion, and optical interference.

Common artefacts and limitations
- Motion artefact: patient movement causes fluctuating light absorption and can create false desaturation or spurious readings. Motion-resistant algorithms and signal processing reduce but do not eliminate this problem.
- Poor perfusion: vasoconstriction (cold extremities, shock, vasopressors), low cardiac output, or peripheral vascular disease reduces the pulsatile signal amplitude, decreasing accuracy and often causing dropouts or erroneous low SpO2 readings.
- Sensor type and site: disposable finger clips, reusable probes, forehead reflectance sensors, and ear probes differ in susceptibility to motion and perfusion. Forehead sensors (reflectance) may perform better during poor peripheral perfusion because forehead perfusion is preserved by central circulation; earlobe sensors may be preferable during vasoconstriction. Probe integrity (dirty or broken sensor, ambient light leakage) produces artefacts.
- Ambient light and electromagnetic interference: strong external light sources, infrared heaters, or electrocautery can cause erroneous readings unless probe shielding is adequate.
- Dyshemoglobinemias: carboxyhemoglobin (CO-Hb) absorbs light similarly to oxyhemoglobin at certain wavelengths, causing SpO2 to overestimate true SaO2 in carbon monoxide poisoning. Methemoglobinemia yields readings around 85% regardless of true oxygenation because absorption characteristics produce a fixed ratio at the two wavelengths used.
- Pigmentation and nail polish: heavy pigmentation or dark nail polish (blue/black) and some nail coatings can lower absorbance and produce false readings. Red and pink polishes less commonly interfere. Removing nail polish or using alternate sites can help.
- Low saturation range: at SpO2 below about 80%, the pulse oximeter calibration is less reliable and device-to-device variability increases.

Clinical implications
Clinicians must understand that SpO2 is a trend monitor rather than an absolute diagnostic test under many conditions. Rapid changes in SpO2 are valuable for detecting acute hypoxemia but should prompt consideration of artefact before escalating therapy. If poor perfusion, motion, suspected CO or methemoglobinemia, or clinical mismatch between SpO2 and patient appearance occur, arterial blood gas with co-oximetry is indicated.

# Arterial blood gas (ABG) interpretation: PaO2, PaCO2, pH, alveolar–arterial gradient

Arterial blood gas analysis provides direct measurement of PaO2, PaCO2, arterial pH, and calculated bicarbonate (HCO3−) and base excess. Integrating these values allows assessment of oxygenation, ventilation, acid–base disturbance, and efficiency of gas exchange.

Normal reference ranges
- pH: 7.35–7.45
- PaCO2: 35–45 mmHg
- PaO2: 80–100 mmHg (on room air at sea level)
- HCO3−: 22–26 mEq/L (mmol/L)
- SaO2: 95–100% (corresponding PaO2 varies with oxyhemoglobin dissociation curve)
These ranges vary with age, altitude, and comorbid conditions.

PaO2 and oxygenation
PaO2 quantifies arterial oxygen tension. Clinical interpretation requires knowledge of the fraction of inspired oxygen (FiO2), altitude, and the alveolar gas equation to identify whether hypoxemia is due to hypoventilation, diffusion impairment, ventilation–perfusion (V/Q) mismatch, or shunt.

Alveolar gas equation and the A–a gradient
The alveolar gas equation expresses alveolar oxygen tension \(PAO_2\):

\(PAO_2 = F_{IO_2} \times (P_{b} - P_{H_2O}) - \dfrac{PaCO_2}{R}\)

where \(P_b\) is barometric pressure (760 mmHg at sea level), \(P_{H_2O}\) is water vapor pressure at body temperature (47 mmHg), and \(R\) is the respiratory quotient (commonly 0.8). For room air (\(F_{IO_2}=0.21\)) at sea level, a convenient form is:

\(PAO_2 \approx 0.21 \times 713 - \dfrac{PaCO_2}{0.8} \approx 150 - \dfrac{PaCO_2}{0.8}\)

The alveolar–arterial (A–a) gradient is:

\(A\text{-}a\ gradient = PAO_2 - PaO_2\)

Normal A–a gradient increases with age. A practical estimate is:

Expected A–a gradient ≈ \( \dfrac{age}{4} + 4 \) mmHg

An elevated A–a gradient implies impaired oxygen transfer due to V/Q mismatch, diffusion limitation, or intracardiac/intrapulmonary shunt. A normal A–a gradient with hypoxemia suggests hypoventilation or low inspired oxygen.

PaCO2 and ventilation
PaCO2 is a direct measure of alveolar ventilation in steady state. PaCO2 is inversely proportional to minute ventilation:

\(PaCO_2 \propto \dfrac{CO_2 \ production}{alveolar\ ventilation}\)

Acute changes in PaCO2 typically reflect changes in minute ventilation. Chronic respiratory disorders produce renal compensation with changes in bicarbonate.

pH and acid–base interpretation
Interpretation of acid–base status integrates pH, PaCO2, and HCO3−. The Henderson–Hasselbalch equation links pH to these variables. Clinical rules of thumb:

- Acute respiratory acidosis: every 10 mmHg rise in PaCO2 is associated with an acute fall in HCO3− of ~1 mEq/L; chronic (renal compensation) rise increases HCO3− by ~3–4 mEq/L per 10 mmHg.
- Acute respiratory alkalosis: every 10 mmHg fall in PaCO2 reduces HCO3− by ~2 mEq/L acutely; chronic compensation reduces HCO3− by ~4–5 mEq/L per 10 mmHg.
- Metabolic acidosis: expected respiratory compensation (Winter’s formula) PaCO2 ≈ \(1.5 \times [HCO_3^-] + 8 \pm 2\) mmHg.
- Metabolic alkalosis: expected PaCO2 increases by ~0.6–0.7 mmHg per 1 mEq/L rise in HCO3− (acute estimates vary).

Clinical use of ABG
Obtain ABG when there is unexplained hypoxemia, sudden deterioration, suspected acid–base disorder, or need to measure PaCO2 directly (suspected hypoventilation). Always interpret ABG in clinical context, considering oxygen therapy and timing relative to interventions.

# End-tidal and transcutaneous CO2 monitoring: principles and clinical uses

End-tidal CO2 (EtCO2) monitoring and transcutaneous CO2 (PtcCO2) are noninvasive methods to estimate ventilation and are used in different clinical contexts.

End-tidal CO2 (capnography)
Principles: Capnography measures CO2 in exhaled gas, presenting a continuous waveform and a numeric EtCO2 value—the maximum CO2 concentration at end-expiration. Main technologies are mainstream (sensor in airway circuit) and sidestream (small sample aspirated to analyzer). The EtCO2 approximates PaCO2 because CO2 diffuses from pulmonary capillaries into alveoli during expiration and reaches a peak at end-expiration.

Typical relationship and normal values: In healthy lungs, PaCO2 - EtCO2 (the arterial to end-tidal gradient) is usually 2–5 mmHg, with EtCO2 slightly lower than PaCO2. EtCO2 values of 35–45 mmHg are often considered normal in adult ventilated patients.

Clinical uses:
- Verification of endotracheal tube placement immediately after intubation (sustained EtCO2 indicates tracheal intubation).
- Continuous monitoring of ventilation in intubated patients and during procedural sedation or anesthesia.
- Monitoring quality of CPR: presence and waveform trends of EtCO2 during resuscitation correlate with cardiac output and return of spontaneous circulation.
- Detection of ventilatory obstruction, disconnection, rebreathing, or changes in dead space ventilation via changes in waveform shape and EtCO2.

Limitations and factors altering EtCO2–PaCO2 relationship:
- Increased physiologic dead space (pulmonary embolism, low cardiac output, severe V/Q mismatch) widens the PaCO2–EtCO2 gradient; EtCO2 underestimates PaCO2.
- Low pulmonary perfusion (hypotension, cardiac arrest) reduces EtCO2 despite rising PaCO2.
- Rapid changes in ventilation, airway occlusion, leaks, secretions, and faulty sampling can distort readings.
- In nonintubated patients, capnography via nasal cannulae can be influenced by mouth breathing and supplemental oxygen flow; values are less reliable quantitatively but can show trends and presence/absence of ventilation.

Transcutaneous CO2 monitoring
Principles: Transcutaneous monitors measure CO2 tension through the skin using a heated electrode that increases local capillary blood flow and gas diffusion. The device yields continuous PtcCO2 and is widely used in neonates and pediatrics where frequent ABGs are problematic.

Performance and caveats:
- PtcCO2 correlates well with PaCO2 when monitored and calibrated correctly, but there is a time lag (several minutes) to reflect rapid changes.
- Accuracy depends on electrode temperature (often 42–44°C), sensor site, skin thickness, perfusion, and calibration. Skin burns are a rare complication from prolonged heating.
- In low perfusion states, accuracy falls because local capillary blood flow is reduced.
- Regular site changes and calibration against ABG improve reliability.

Clinical considerations
- Choose EtCO2 monitoring for real-time ventilatory monitoring during anesthesia, intubation, and CPR. Use EtCO2 waveforms to detect airway and ventilatory problems.
- Use transcutaneous CO2 for continuous monitoring in neonates, children, and in certain critical care settings when frequent ABGs are impractical, remembering the lag and need for calibration.
- When precise PaCO2 is required (e.g., in unstable physiology, major acid–base disorders), obtain ABG with direct PaCO2 measurement.

# Nocturnal oximetry: indications, limitations and interpretation

Nocturnal oximetry is a simple, low-cost test that records SpO2 over sleep and provides indices of nocturnal hypoxemia. It is commonly used as a screening tool, not a definitive diagnostic test for sleep-disordered breathing.

Indications
- Screening for obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) when full polysomnography is not immediately available or when pretest probability is high.
- Monitoring oxygen desaturation in patients with chronic lung disease (COPD), neuromuscular disease, or obesity hypoventilation syndrome.
- Evaluating nocturnal oxygenation in infants, children, or high-risk postoperative patients.
- Assessing the need for nocturnal supplemental oxygen or monitoring response to therapy when pulmonary function tests and clinical context suggest risk.

Interpretation and indices
Commonly reported metrics include mean SpO2, lowest SpO2, time spent below specified thresholds (e.g., T90: percent of sleep with SpO2 <90%), and the oxygen desaturation index (ODI), which is the number of desaturation events per hour of recording—typically defined as drops in SpO2 of ≥3% or ≥4% from baseline depending on protocol. ODI thresholds vary by center; an ODI ≥5 events/hour can be considered abnormal; higher values suggest more severe disease.

Limitations
- Oximetry cannot differentiate central versus obstructive apneas, nor does it detect airflow or respiratory effort—so it cannot diagnose OSA definitively.
- Desaturation events may be missed if sampling frequency is low or if averaging times smooth brief dips.
- Motion artefacts during sleep, poor sensor placement, sensor detachment, and poor peripheral perfusion reduce data quality and can produce false positives or negatives.
- Overnight oximetry underestimates hypercapnia: patients may desaturate minimally but develop significant nocturnal hypoventilation with hypercapnia (e.g., obesity hypoventilation, neuromuscular weakness).
- Intermittent or transient arousals that cause brief hypoventilation may not produce prolonged desaturations but can be clinically relevant.

Clinical application
Nocturnal oximetry is most useful as a screening tool: a normal result does not exclude sleep-disordered breathing, particularly in patients suspected of primary hypoventilation or those on supplemental oxygen. Abnormal recordings guide the need for comprehensive testing with polysomnography or for measurement of nocturnal PaCO2 or transcutaneous CO2 in suspected hypoventilation.

# Relationship between oxygen saturation, PaO2 and hypoventilation

The oxyhemoglobin dissociation curve describes the nonlinear relationship between PaO2 and hemoglobin oxygen saturation (SaO2). Small changes in PaO2 produce large changes in SaO2 on the steep portion of the curve (PaO2 <60 mmHg), while on the flat portion (PaO2 >60 mmHg) large changes in PaO2 affect SaO2 minimally.

Key features
- At PaO2 ≈ 100 mmHg, SaO2 ≈ 98–100%.
- At PaO2 ≈ 60 mmHg, SaO2 ≈ 90%—this is an inflection point clinically considered the threshold for significant hypoxemia.
- The sigmoid shape means that above PaO2 ≈ 60 mmHg, SpO2 falls slowly with decreasing PaO2; below that, small falls in PaO2 lead to large SaO2 decreases.

Factors shifting the curve
Shifts to the right (facilitating oxygen unloading to tissues, i.e., lower affinity): increased temperature, increased PaCO2, decreased pH (acidosis), increased 2,3-DPG. Shifts to the left (increased affinity): hypothermia, decreased PaCO2, alkalosis, decreased 2,3-DPG, fetal hemoglobin.

Hypoventilation and its effect on PaO2 and SaO2
Hypoventilation causes an increase in PaCO2 (hypercapnia) and, by the alveolar gas equation, a decrease in PAO2 and therefore lower PaO2. The magnitude of hypoxemia depends on FiO2 and the baseline A–a gradient. In a patient breathing room air with normal A–a gradient, hypoventilation reduces PAO2 and thus PaO2 predictably. However, because of the flat portion of the dissociation curve, moderate reductions in PaO2 (e.g., from 100 to 80 mmHg) may produce little change in SaO2, and pulse oximetry may remain near-normal. Only when PaO2 falls below ~60 mmHg will SpO2 fall to <90% and be clearly abnormal.

Supplemental oxygen confounds this relationship: supplemental O2 raises PAO2 and PaO2 even in the presence of hypoventilation, so SpO2 may remain normal despite significant hypercapnia. This dissociation is a central cause of clinical scenarios where pulse oximetry appears reassuring while ventilation is inadequate.

Clinical takeaways
Pulse oximetry measures oxygenation (SaO2) but does not measure ventilation (PaCO2). Because of the shape of the oxyhemoglobin dissociation curve, hypoventilation may not cause a proportional drop in SpO2 until severe hypoxemia has developed, especially if supplemental oxygen is present. Detecting hypoventilation sometimes requires direct PaCO2 measurement via ABG, transcutaneous CO2 monitoring, or capnography.

# End-tidal and transcutaneous CO2 monitoring: principles and clinical uses (revisited with respect to hypoventilation)

(Combined exposition to emphasize ventilation monitoring.)

Monitoring carbon dioxide provides direct or surrogate measures of alveolar ventilation. The two main bedside surrogates are EtCO2 and PtcCO2. Both can detect hypoventilation earlier than pulse oximetry in many cases because rising PaCO2 often precedes significant hypoxemia.

EtCO2 in detecting hypoventilation
Continuous capnography provides immediate feedback about ventilation: progressive rise in EtCO2 or a decrease to zero (apnea) indicates hypoventilation or loss of airway patency. In patients breathing room air without major V/Q mismatch, EtCO2 trends mirror PaCO2 trends closely enough to detect acute hypoventilation. However, when physiologic dead space is increased (e.g., COPD, pulmonary embolism), EtCO2 may underestimate PaCO2 and fail to reveal the full magnitude of hypercapnia.

Transcutaneous CO2 in detecting hypoventilation
Transcutaneous CO2 measures rising CO2 in the skin capillaries and provides a continuous numeric estimate more directly related to PaCO2 than EtCO2 in nonintubated patients. It is particularly useful for overnight monitoring in patients at risk for hypoventilation (obesity hypoventilation syndrome, neuromuscular disorders) where intermittent ABGs are impractical. PtcCO2 lags behind rapid changes in PaCO2 but detects sustained hypoventilation earlier than nocturnal oximetry.

When to use which
- In intubated or mechanically ventilated patients, capnography is standard for confirmation of tube placement and continuous ventilatory monitoring.
- In sedated but spontaneously breathing patients, combined pulse oximetry and capnography is superior to pulse oximetry alone for early detection of hypoventilation and apnea.
- For nonintubated patients at risk for nocturnal hypoventilation, transcutaneous CO2 monitoring provides important information not available from oximetry alone.

# Nocturnal oximetry limitations and detection of hypoventilation

Reiterating the specific problem: nocturnal oximetry measures desaturation events but does not measure ventilation directly. Patients with nocturnal hypoventilation may maintain near-normal SpO2 for long periods—especially if they receive supplemental oxygen or have preserved oxygenation due to right-shifted oxyhemoglobin curve compensations—while developing significant hypercapnia. In obesity hypoventilation syndrome and neuromuscular disease, incremental increases in PaCO2 during sleep (particularly during REM when accessory muscle activity falls) may not produce large desaturations but will increase PaCO2 and cause daytime somnolence, morning headache, and cognitive changes. Therefore, in patients at risk for nocturnal hypercapnia, overnight monitoring should include transcutaneous CO2 or full polysomnography with capnometry rather than oximetry alone.

# Clinical scenarios where pulse oximetry may miss hypoventilation (normoxia with hypercapnia)

Understanding scenarios where SpO2 appears reassuring while PaCO2 is elevated is critical to avoid missed respiratory failure.

1. Opioid or sedative-induced hypoventilation
Opioids depress central respiratory drive, leading to alveolar hypoventilation and rising PaCO2. If the patient is receiving supplemental oxygen, SpO2 may remain within normal limits while CO2 accumulates. Clinical clues include somnolence, decreased respiratory rate, and shallow breathing. Reliance on SpO2 alone is dangerous in this context; capnography or ABG measurement should be used for monitoring.

2. Obesity hypoventilation syndrome (OHS)
Patients with OHS may have chronic hypercapnia with near-normal oxygenation when awake or on supplemental oxygen. Nocturnal hypoventilation, particularly during REM sleep, can lead to progressive hypercapnia without marked desaturation. Overnight PtcCO2 monitoring or polysomnography with CO2 measurements is required to detect hypoventilation.

3. Neuromuscular weakness (amyotrophic lateral sclerosis, myasthenia gravis, muscular dystrophies)
Weak inspiratory muscles reduce minute ventilation, causing hypercapnia that can be out of proportion to hypoxemia until late in the disease. Early hypoventilation symptoms are subtle; forced vital capacity measurements and serial ABGs or PtcCO2 measurements are important.

4. Advanced COPD with chronic CO2 retention
In some COPD patients, chronic hypercapnia coexists with relatively preserved SpO2, particularly if supplemental oxygen is given. Acute on chronic hypercapnia can occur without large changes in SpO2. Additionally, increased physiologic dead space in COPD widens the PaCO2–EtCO2 gradient, making EtCO2 less reliable unless calibrated.

5. Carbon monoxide poisoning and dyshemoglobinemias
CO poisoning produces falsely normal or high SpO2 because pulse oximetry cannot distinguish carboxyhemoglobin from oxyhemoglobin. Although this situation is not hypercapnia per se, it represents another important scenario where SpO2 is misleading.

6. Supplemental oxygen therapy
Any situation where FiO2 is raised (oxygen therapy, high-flow nasal cannula) can mask hypoventilation on pulse oximetry by increasing PaO2 and SaO2 while PaCO2 rises. This is particularly important in the emergency department and postoperative settings.

Clinical decision points
- Do not rely on SpO2 alone to assess ventilation. When clinical concern for hypoventilation exists—changes in mental status, over-sedation, morning headache, daytime somnolence, or risk factors as above—obtain ABG for PaCO2, use capnography for immediate monitoring in sedated or intubated patients, and consider PtcCO2 for continuous noninvasive monitoring.
- Remove supplemental oxygen briefly (with caution) to assess baseline oxygenation and ventilation when clinically appropriate and safe; this may unmask hypoventilation but must be done under controlled conditions.
- In the perioperative and procedural sedation setting, combined monitoring (pulse oximetry + capnography) is standard of care to detect apnea and hypoventilation earlier than oximetry alone.

# Summary and practical approach

- Pulse oximetry is an invaluable, continuous monitor of arterial hemoglobin oxygen saturation but is limited by motion, poor perfusion, dyshemoglobinemias, sensor issues, and the nonlinear relation between PaO2 and SaO2. It measures oxygenation, not ventilation.
- ABG provides definitive measurement of PaO2, PaCO2, pH, and HCO3−. Use the alveolar gas equation and the A–a gradient to distinguish hypoventilation from V/Q mismatch, diffusion impairment, and shunt.
- Capnography (EtCO2) is the standard real-time monitor of ventilation in intubated and sedated patients; it also helps confirm tube placement and guide resuscitation. EtCO2 correlates with PaCO2 under many conditions but underestimates PaCO2 when dead space is increased or pulmonary blood flow is low.
- Transcutaneous CO2 monitoring gives continuous, noninvasive estimates of PaCO2 useful in neonates, children, and patients at risk for nocturnal hypoventilation; it has a time lag and must be calibrated.
- Nocturnal oximetry screens for desaturation but cannot reliably detect nocturnal hypercapnia and cannot distinguish central from obstructive events. For suspected nocturnal hypoventilation, use polysomnography with CO2 monitoring or transcutaneous CO2.
- Be vigilant for clinical scenarios in which SpO2 can be normal despite dangerous hypercapnia: opioid/sedative depression, obesity hypoventilation, neuromuscular weakness, COPD with chronic CO2 retention, and whenever supplemental oxygen is used.

In clinical practice, choose monitoring modalities based on the physiologic question: oxygenation (ABG, pulse oximetry, A–a gradient assessment), ventilation (ABG PaCO2, capnography, transcutaneous CO2), and the need for trend versus absolute accuracy. Combine methods when necessary to avoid dangerous blind spots—particularly the common pitfall of normoxia with unrecognized hypoventilation.

Introduction

Pulmonary function testing (PFT) is an indispensable set of bedside and laboratory tools for evaluating dyspnea, ventilatory failure, and suspected respiratory muscle impairment. In patients with neuromuscular disease (NMD) or central causes of hypoventilation, the dominant problem is inadequate generation of ventilatory pressure and volumes rather than primary abnormalities of airways or alveolar gas-exchange. Correct interpretation of standard spirometry, static lung volumes, diffusion capacity, and specific measures of respiratory muscle strength is therefore essential to distinguish primary pulmonary disease from neuromuscular or central causes of dyspnea and to determine timing for interventions such as noninvasive ventilation (NIV). This chapter presents a clinically oriented synthesis of the physiological bases of common PFT measurements, practical performance points, normative values and diagnostic criteria, patterns seen in neuromuscular weakness versus primary lung disease, special tests of diaphragm and inspiratory function, and monitoring thresholds that influence clinical decision-making.

# Standard spirometry: FEV1, FVC, FEV1/FVC ratio and patterns (restrictive vs obstructive)

Spirometry is the foundational pulmonary function test and measures dynamic airflow during forced expiratory maneuvers. Key indices are forced expiratory volume in 1 second (FEV1), forced vital capacity (FVC), and the ratio FEV1/FVC. FEV1 is the volume exhaled in the first second of a maximal forced expiration started from full inspiration; FVC is the total volume exhaled during that maneuver. The FEV1/FVC ratio reflects the proportion of lung volume that can be exhaled quickly and is central to distinguishing obstructive from non-obstructive defects.

Interpretation routinely uses absolute values, percent predicted (based on age, sex, height, ethnicity), and lower limits of normal (LLN). An FEV1/FVC below the LLN (or often simplified as <0.70 in adults) indicates airflow obstruction. Severity of obstruction is commonly staged by percent predicted FEV1 (e.g., mild ≥80%, moderate 50–79%, severe 30–49%, very severe <30%), although disease-specific guidelines differ.

Restrictive physiology on spirometry is suggested when FVC is reduced but the FEV1/FVC ratio is normal or increased (because both FEV1 and FVC are proportionately reduced). Spirometry alone, however, cannot confirm restriction because a reduced FVC may reflect poor effort, air-trapping with small airway obstruction, or technical factors. Confirmation requires measurement of static lung volumes (total lung capacity, TLC). Typical numerical cues: an isolated FVC reduction with normal/increased FEV1/FVC should prompt lung volume testing.

In neuromuscular weakness, the common spirometric pattern is a proportional reduction in FVC and FEV1 with preserved or increased FEV1/FVC ratio. For example, as inspiratory and expiratory muscle strength declines, FVC falls because maximal inflation is limited, and FEV1 falls in proportion because the lungs contain less volume to exhale. This pattern mimics restrictive disease on spirometry but differs mechanistically: parenchymal restriction reduces TLC and often produces reduced DLCO (diffusing capacity), whereas NMD primarily reduces respiratory muscle capacity with relatively preserved lung parenchyma and DLCO (unless coexisting pulmonary disease). Note that small airways disease may also produce a low FVC with normal ratio (pseudo-restriction) — hence the importance of full lung volumes.

Technical and clinical considerations: ensure maximal and reproducible effort, use seated and supine measurements when diaphragmatic weakness is suspected (a fall in FVC of >10–25% from seated to supine suggests diaphragmatic dysfunction), and interpret values relative to predicted using appropriate reference equations. In chronic neuromuscular disease, progressive declines in FVC over serial testing are often the most informative signal of disease progression.

# Maximal inspiratory and expiratory pressures (MIP/MEP) as markers of respiratory muscle strength

Maximal inspiratory pressure (MIP) and maximal expiratory pressure (MEP) are simple volitional measures of global inspiratory and expiratory muscle strength measured at the mouth. MIP (sometimes called PImax) is measured from residual volume with the subject performing a maximal inspiratory effort against an occluded airway; MEP (PEmax) is measured from total lung capacity with a maximal expiratory effort. Both are expressed in cmH2O and are highly reproducible when performed correctly.

Physiological significance: MIP primarily reflects the strength of the diaphragm and other inspiratory muscles (external intercostals, accessory muscles), while MEP predominantly reflects abdominal and internal intercostal muscle strength needed for cough and secretion clearance. Because the tests are effort-dependent, coaching and repeatable trials are required; the highest value from at least three acceptable efforts generally is recorded.

Reference values and interpretation: Normal ranges vary with age, sex, and methodology. Typical adult reference ranges are approximately:
- MIP: men about -80 to -120 cmH2O, women about -60 to -80 cmH2O (values are negative because they are measured relative to atmospheric pressure with inspiratory negative pressure).
- MEP: men about +120 to +200 cmH2O, women about +80 to +140 cmH2O.

Because of population variance and technique sensitivity, interpretation uses percent predicted and clinical thresholds. Common interpretive cut-points in neuromuscular practice:
- MIP (more positive/less negative) greater than about -60 to -80 cmH2O is generally considered acceptable; values less negative than -60 cmH2O suggest clinically significant inspiratory muscle weakness, and values less negative than -30 cmH2O indicate severe weakness associated with risk of hypoventilation and respiratory failure.
- MEP values less than +60–80 cmH2O indicate impaired expiratory muscle strength and an impaired ability to cough or clear secretions; MEP <40 cmH2O denotes high risk for ineffective cough.

Limitations and practical tips: Because MIP/MEP require patient cooperation and appropriate technique, low results must be interpreted in context. False low values can result from submaximal effort, poor mouth seal, or facial weakness; in bulbar dysfunction, oral pressures may be underestimated. Nonvolitional tests (phrenic nerve stimulation, transdiaphragmatic pressures) can be considered when volitional measures are unreliable.

Clinical utility: MIP is useful for screening and serial monitoring in NMD, for prognostication, and for timing of interventions (e.g., initiation of NIV, cough augmentation). MEP identifies patients at risk for ineffective cough and guides use of mechanical in-exsufflation or assisted cough devices.

# Indications for full lung volumes and diffusion capacity testing

Full pulmonary function testing beyond spirometry includes measurement of static lung volumes (total lung capacity, residual volume) and single-breath diffusing capacity for carbon monoxide (DLCO). These tests are indicated whenever spirometry suggests restriction, when there is discordance between clinical assessment and spirometry, or when assessment of diffusion or air-trapping is required.

Indications in the context of neuromuscular and central causes include:
- Confirmation of restriction suggested by reduced FVC and normal/increased FEV1/FVC on spirometry. Measurement of TLC is required to confirm true restrictive lung disease (TLC < LLN).
- Differentiation of parenchymal restrictive disease (e.g., pulmonary fibrosis) from extrapulmonary or neuromuscular causes. In parenchymal restriction, both TLC and DLCO are typically reduced (DLCO often reduced to a greater degree than expected), whereas isolated respiratory muscle weakness typically causes decreased TLC with relatively preserved DLCO.
- Quantification of air-trapping or hyperinflation when small airways disease or COPD is a consideration. Elevated residual volume or increased RV/TLC ratio suggests obstructive physiology with gas trapping.
- Assessment of diffusion capacity (DLCO) when gas-exchange impairment is suspected (e.g., interstitial lung disease, pulmonary vascular disease). DLCO is a sensitive indicator of alveolar-capillary membrane or pulmonary vascular pathology and is often preserved in pure neuromuscular disease unless there is concomitant lung disease.
- Preoperative assessment prior to major surgery in patients with NMD, when accurate evaluation of ventilatory reserve is necessary.

Interpretation essentials: TLC confirms restriction; TLC < LLN establishes restrictive physiology. Typical normal TLC values vary by sex, height, and age; percent predicted and LLN values from reference equations should be used. DLCO is usually reported as ml/min/mmHg and as percent predicted; normal DLCO typically is around 80–120% predicted, with LLN commonly at about 80% predicted depending on the equation. A reduced DLCO (<60–70% predicted or below LLN) suggests significant parenchymal or vascular disease. KCO (DLCO corrected for alveolar volume) can help distinguish reduced DLCO due to reduced alveolar volume vs impaired membrane conductance.

Testing modalities: Lung volumes can be measured by body plethysmography (preferred, especially when obstruction is present), helium dilution, or nitrogen washout. DLCO requires appropriate correction for hemoglobin and carboxyhemoglobin; anemia reduces DLCO and must be accounted for.

# Interpretation of tests in neuromuscular weakness versus primary pulmonary disease

Distinguishing neuromuscular respiratory impairment from primary pulmonary disease requires integrating spirometry, lung volumes, DLCO, respiratory muscle testing, clinical features, and imaging.

Pattern recognition:
- Neuromuscular disease: Characterized by reduced FVC and TLC reflecting inability to inflate the chest, with a preserved or increased FEV1/FVC ratio. DLCO is typically normal or near-normal when expressed per unit alveolar volume (KCO may be preserved), because the alveolar-capillary membrane is intact. Chest imaging (radiograph/CT) is often unremarkable for parenchymal disease, though chronic atelectasis from hypoventilation may be present. Supine FVC falls relative to upright FVC; a decline of >10–25% is strongly suggestive of diaphragmatic weakness. MIP and MEP are reduced. Patients may have hypoventilation (hypercapnia) especially during sleep, with nocturnal desaturation and morning headaches.
- Primary restrictive lung disease (interstitial lung disease): Presents with reduced TLC and FVC, often disproportionately reduced DLCO (reflecting decreased alveolar-capillary surface area), and radiographic/CT evidence of parenchymal fibrosis. FEV1/FVC ratio may be normal or increased. Gas-exchange impairment (hypoxemia) is often present at rest or with exertion. MIP/MEP may be preserved or only secondarily reduced due to deconditioning.
- Obstructive disease (COPD, asthma): Shows reduced FEV1, reduced FEV1/FVC ratio below LLN, and air-trapping with increased residual volume and possible hyperinflation (increased TLC in emphysema). DLCO is decreased in emphysema. Spirometric response to bronchodilator may help distinguish asthma from fixed obstruction.

Key clinical decision points:
- When spirometry shows reduced FVC with preserved ratio, confirm TLC by plethysmography to distinguish true restriction from pseudo-restriction due to airflow obstruction.
- If DLCO is normal and TLC is reduced with low MIP/MEP, neuromuscular causes are favored.
- Evaluate the seated-to-supine FVC change: a substantial fall supports diaphragmatic involvement.
- Consider arterial blood gas or capillary blood gas measurement to document hypercapnia (PaCO2 >45 mmHg) in ventilatory failure; nocturnal capnography or polysomnography may detect sleep hypoventilation earlier than daytime hypercapnia.

# Role of sniff nasal inspiratory pressure and diaphragmatic function tests

Sniff nasal inspiratory pressure (SNIP) and more direct measures of diaphragmatic function provide additional, sometimes more sensitive tests of inspiratory muscle and diaphragmatic performance, particularly when MIP is difficult to obtain (e.g., bulbar weakness, poor mouth seal, facial weakness).

SNIP: SNIP measures the negative pressure generated in the nasal cavity during a maximal sniff through an unobstructed nostril, using a small pressure probe. It is less dependent on lip/mouth seal and often better tolerated. Normal SNIP values again vary by sex and age; approximate thresholds used clinically:
- Normal SNIP: men often >60–80 cmH2O, women >40–60 cmH2O.
- SNIP <40 cmH2O is commonly regarded as indicative of inspiratory muscle weakness and correlates with risk of nocturnal hypoventilation in neuromuscular disease.

SNIP has particular value in amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS) and other NMDs where MIP may be underestimated. SNIP correlates with MIP and with transdiaphragmatic pressure in many studies.

Diaphragmatic function tests: Several techniques assess diaphragmatic strength and motion more directly:
- Maximal transdiaphragmatic pressure (Pdi) measured by placing pressure transducers in the esophagus and stomach and calculating the difference during maximal inspiratory effort or magnetic/phrenic nerve stimulation. Pdi is the gold-standard physiologic measure of diaphragmatic force but is invasive and mainly used in research or specialized centers.
- Twitch transdiaphragmatic pressure during phrenic nerve stimulation (electrical or magnetic) provides a nonvolitional assessment of diaphragmatic contractility and can confirm weakness when volitional tests are equivocal.
- Ultrasound assessment of diaphragmatic motion and thickening fraction provides noninvasive bedside assessment: reduced diaphragmatic excursion on sniff or quiet breathing, or a low inspiratory thickening fraction (percentage change in diaphragm thickness between end-expiration and end-inspiration), supports diaphragmatic dysfunction. Typical cutoffs vary; a thickening fraction <20–30% is often considered abnormal, though normative values depend on methodology and patient population.
- Fluoroscopic "sniff test" (radiographic observation of paradoxical diaphragmatic motion) historically used for unilateral paralysis, but less commonly performed now because of ultrasound availability.

Clinical utility: Use SNIP or diaphragmatic ultrasound when mouth pressures are unreliable, when you need to detect early inspiratory muscle involvement, or to evaluate asymptomatic patients with neuromuscular disorders. A falling SNIP or a declining diaphragmatic thickening fraction over serial measurements indicates progressive diaphragmatic dysfunction and should prompt consideration of sleep studies and ventilatory support.

# Serial monitoring and thresholds that prompt ventilatory support

Respiratory function in neuromuscular and central disorders often declines gradually, and serial testing provides the best insight into progression and timing for interventions. Regular monitoring intervals depend on disease trajectory: rapidly progressive conditions (e.g., ALS) may require assessments every 1–3 months, whereas more slowly progressive disorders might be monitored every 6–12 months.

Key measurements to follow serially:
- FVC in upright and supine positions. A decline in FVC over time is a robust marker of disease progression. Significant changes are often defined as a fall of ≥10% absolute or ≥20% relative decline in FVC over a period (use same technique and effort).
- MIP/MEP and SNIP—declining pressures indicate progressive respiratory muscle impairment.
- Nocturnal oxygen saturation and transcutaneous or end-tidal CO2 monitoring to detect nocturnal hypoventilation before daytime hypercapnia ensues.
- Cough peak flow and MEP to assess ability to clear secretions.

Common clinical thresholds that prompt consideration of NIV, cough-assist devices, or further evaluation:
- Vital capacity (VC or FVC) falling below 50% of predicted is a widely used threshold to evaluate for nocturnal hypoventilation and to consider baseline respiratory support and sleep studies. Some centers use absolute VC <1.0 L or <20 mL/kg as a practical indicator of risk for respiratory failure.
- A reduction in supine FVC by >25% relative to upright FVC strongly suggests diaphragmatic weakness and warrants prompt consideration of sleep-disordered breathing evaluation.
- MIP less negative than -60 cmH2O indicates significant inspiratory weakness; values less negative than -30 cmH2O are associated with high risk of ventilatory failure. MEP <60–80 cmH2O or cough peak flow <160 L/min (some use <270 L/min depending on source) indicates ineffective cough and the need for cough augmentation strategies; a cough peak flow <160 L/min is often considered the threshold below which secretion clearance is ineffective and mechanical cough assistance should be provided.
- Evidence of nocturnal hypoventilation—commonly defined as transcutaneous or end-tidal CO2 >55 mmHg for ≥10 minutes, or sustained increases from daytime baseline, or sustained oxygen desaturations during sleep—should prompt initiation of nocturnal NIV even in the absence of daytime hypercapnia.
- Daytime hypercapnia (PaCO2 >45–50 mmHg) or hypoxemia that is exertional or at rest indicates respiratory pump failure and requires urgent assessment for ventilatory support.
- Symptoms such as orthopnea, morning headache, unrefreshing sleep, daytime somnolence, and cognitive impairment alongside objective declines in FVC or MIP increase the urgency to consider NIV.

Clinical decision-making must be individualized. For example, progressively symptomatic patients with FVC in the range 50–80% predicted but with orthopnea, declining SNIP, or early nocturnal hypercapnia may need earlier initiation of nocturnal NIV. Conversely, asymptomatic patients with mildly reduced FVC may be observed more conservatively with frequent reassessment.

# Integration into clinical practice and practical considerations

Interpreting PFTs in patients with neuromuscular or central hypoventilation involves synthesizing test results with clinical presentation, imaging, and blood gas measurements. Important practical points include:
- Always consider effort and technique: poor effort, air leaks, or facial/bulbar weakness can invalidate mouth pressure measurements. Supplement volitional tests with SNIP, ultrasound, or phrenic nerve stimulation when appropriate.
- Measure seated and supine FVC to detect diaphragmatic weakness.
- Use body plethysmography to confirm restriction and quantify residual volume when obstruction or air-trapping is a possibility.
- Adjust DLCO for hemoglobin and smoking status when interpreting results.
- Monitor trends rather than isolated values; serial declines often matter more than single measurements.
- Combine objective thresholds (e.g., VC <50% predicted, MIP less negative than -60 cmH2O, SNIP <40 cmH2O, cough peak flow <160 L/min) with symptoms and nocturnal studies to time initiation of NIV and secretion management strategies.

Summary

Pulmonary function testing provides a spectrum of tools—from simple spirometry and mouth pressures to advanced lung volume, diffusion, and diaphragmatic assessments—that allow clinicians to distinguish between primary pulmonary disease and ventilatory pump failure due to neuromuscular or central causes, to detect early hypoventilation (especially during sleep), and to time interventions. Understanding the physiology behind FEV1, FVC, TLC, DLCO, MIP, MEP, SNIP, and more direct measures of diaphragmatic function is essential. Regular, standardized serial monitoring and attention to established thresholds (VC, MIP/MEP, SNIP, cough peak flow, nocturnal CO2) form the backbone of respiratory care in patients at risk of respiratory muscle failure.

# Introduction

Dysphagia, or difficulty swallowing, is a common and potentially serious symptom in neurological disease. Its consequences include malnutrition, dehydration, aspiration pneumonia, and reduced quality of life. Neurologic disorders produce dysphagia through disruption of the complex, coordinated sensorimotor events required for safe and efficient transfer of a bolus from the oral cavity to the stomach. Understanding the distinction between oropharyngeal and esophageal dysphagia, the patterns that arise from cranial nerve and brainstem lesions, and the appropriate use and interpretation of diagnostic tools is essential for acute management and long-term rehabilitation planning. This chapter focuses on neurogenic mechanisms of oropharyngeal dysphagia and evidence-based approaches to evaluation and initial management.

# Distinction between oropharyngeal and esophageal dysphagia

Accurate localization of the level of dysfunction—whether oral, pharyngeal, or esophageal—is the first step in assessment. Oropharyngeal dysphagia refers to impaired transfer of food from the mouth through the pharynx into the esophagus. It typically presents with immediate difficulty initiating a swallow, coughing or choking during or shortly after swallowing, nasal regurgitation, a wet or gurgly voice, and multiple swallows for a single bolus. Patients may report pooling of saliva or food in the mouth, drooling, or choking on liquids more than solids. Symptoms often occur at the start of the swallow and are associated with airway compromise (aspiration).

Esophageal dysphagia refers to perceived obstruction or delay in passage of a bolus through the esophagus. Clinical features include sensation of food "sticking" in the chest or throat after swallowing, retrosternal discomfort, regurgitation of undigested food, and symptoms that occur after a variable delay following the act of swallowing rather than immediately. Esophageal dysphagia may be progressive (mechanical obstruction like stricture or malignancy) or intermittent (motility disorders such as achalasia, diffuse esophageal spasm).

Distinguishing features useful at the bedside include timing of symptoms relative to swallowing (immediate suggests oropharyngeal), association with coughing/aspiration (common in oropharyngeal), and whether both solids and liquids are affected (neurogenic or motility disorders often affect both; mechanical obstruction usually begins with solids). Examination findings supportive of oropharyngeal dysphagia include drooling, poor lip closure, impaired tongue movement, nasal regurgitation, and a wet voice on phonation or after swallowing. It is important to remember that mixed presentations occur, and patients may have both oropharyngeal and esophageal components; appropriate imaging and endoscopic evaluation are required when the clinical picture is unclear.

# Neurogenic dysphagia patterns related to cranial nerve and brainstem lesions

Swallowing is controlled by a distributed network that includes cortical and subcortical regions, the brainstem central pattern generator (CPG) for swallowing, cranial nerve nuclei, peripheral cranial nerves, and the muscles of the oropharynx and upper esophageal sphincter (UES). Neurogenic dysphagia may result from lesions at any level. Recognizing pattern-recognition clusters helps localize pathology and tailor management.

Cortical and corticobulbar pathway lesions, such as unilateral or bilateral strokes in the sensorimotor cortex or internal capsule, commonly produce supranuclear dysphagia. These lesions often cause deficits in voluntary initiation of swallowing, delayed pharyngeal swallow trigger, reduced bolus control, and impaired coordination, particularly of the oral and pharyngeal phases. Because reflexive components mediated by the brainstem may be partially preserved, signs can include inefficient mastication, pocketing of food in the buccal sulcus, and delayed laryngeal elevation leading to aspiration, often with thin liquids. Cortical lesions typically cause contralateral weakness but bilateral involvement is common and increases severity. Speech deficits (dysarthria, apraxia of speech) often coexist and aid localization.

Brainstem lesions, particularly those affecting the medulla and lower pons, can directly disrupt the swallowing CPG and cranial nerve nuclei (nucleus ambiguus, nucleus tractus solitarius). Lateral medullary (Wallenberg) infarcts characteristically produce severe pharyngeal dysphagia with diminished pharyngeal constriction, reduced laryngeal elevation, absent gag reflex, and high risk of silent aspiration due to impaired laryngeal closure. These patients frequently have sensory loss in the pharynx and face, resulting in reduced swallow reflex. Bilateral lesions or lesions involving both sensory and motor nuclei can cause profound dysphagia requiring alternative feeding.

Cranial nerve palsies produce predictable deficits according to the nerve(s) involved. Peripheral lesions of the trigeminal nerve (CN V) impair mastication and bolus formation due to weakened jaw closure and lateral movement. Facial nerve (CN VII) lesions cause poor lip seal and buccal incompetence, resulting in anterior spillage and drooling. Glossopharyngeal (CN IX) dysfunction reduces pharyngeal sensation and contributes to diminished swallow trigger and impaired pharyngeal constriction. Vagus nerve (CN X) lesions affect palatal elevation, pharyngeal constriction, laryngeal elevation and closure, and UES relaxation through impairment of pharyngeal and laryngeal muscles, leading to nasal regurgitation, hoarseness, and aspiration. Hypoglossal nerve (CN XII) palsy results in tongue weakness and atrophy, causing inefficient bolus control, oral residue, and difficulty in propelling the bolus posteriorly.

Subcortical structures and basal ganglia involvement, as in Parkinson disease, progressive supranuclear palsy, or Huntington disease, produce characteristic dysphagia patterns. Parkinsonian dysphagia often manifests with bradykinesia and rigidity of orofacial and pharyngeal muscles, delayed swallow initiation, reduced tongue base retraction and pharyngeal contraction, decreased hyolaryngeal excursion, and impaired UES opening. These changes predispose to both oropharyngeal residue and aspiration, particularly with secretions and liquids. Response to dopaminergic therapy is variable, and disease progression results in increasing aspiration risk.

Neuromuscular junction disorders (myasthenia gravis) and primary muscle diseases (inflammatory myopathies, muscular dystrophies) produce fatigable or progressive weakness of the muscles required for swallowing. In myasthenia gravis, dysphagia may fluctuate, worsen with fatigue, and involve both bulbar and proximal limb muscles. Aspiration may follow prolonged chewing and repetitive swallows. Myopathies often cause weakness of tongue and pharyngeal muscles with associated drooling, dysarthria, and risk of aspiration of solids and liquids.

Peripheral neuropathies, such as those seen in motor neuron disease (amyotrophic lateral sclerosis), produce combined upper and lower motor neuron signs. Bulbar-onset ALS typically causes progressive dysphagia with tongue fasciculations, atrophy, reduced palatal movement, and impaired pharyngeal contraction. The risk of aspiration increases steadily, and loss of effective cough contributes to severe pulmonary complications.

Clinical reasoning requires mapping observed swallowing deficits to underlying anatomy and physiology: problems with bolus formation and containment point to oral phase and CN V/VII/CN XII dysfunction; delayed swallow initiation and pharyngeal residue imply sensory or motor impairment of CN IX/X or brainstem CPG; impaired laryngeal elevation and UES opening indicate dysfunction of suprahyoid muscles and central coordination. Recognizing whether aspiration is “pre-swallow” (due to poor airway protection before the swallow), “during swallow” (due to incomplete laryngeal closure), or “post-swallow” (due to pharyngeal residue) helps identify the lesion and relates directly to management choices.

# Modified barium swallow (videofluoroscopy): indications and interpretation (aspiration, penetration, pharyngeal transit)

The modified barium swallow (MBS), commonly referred to as videofluoroscopic swallow study (VFSS), is the gold-standard dynamic radiographic assessment of the oral and pharyngeal phases of swallowing.
