It provides real-time visualization of bolus flow, tongue and pharyngeal movements, laryngeal elevation, UES opening, and airway protection mechanisms.

Indications for VFSS include unexplained cough or choking with swallowing, suspected aspiration (including silent aspiration), progressive dysphagia where localization is uncertain, preoperative assessment when upper airway or pharyngeal anatomy will be altered, failure to progress with therapy, assessment of response to compensatory maneuvers and postural adjustments, and medico-legal documentation. VFSS is particularly useful when clinical bedside assessments are equivocal, when aspiration risk must be quantified, or when the clinician needs to evaluate the effectiveness of rehabilitation strategies.

Technical aspects: VFSS requires a radiology suite with fluoroscopy and a speech-language pathologist or swallowing-trained clinician present. The study should include a range of bolus consistencies and volumes (thin liquids, nectar-thick, honey-thick, puree/pudding, soft solids, and regular solids) as tolerated, with lateral and antero-posterior views to assess symmetry and identify unilateral pharyngeal weakness. Standardized protocols, such as the Modified Barium Swallow Impairment Profile (MBSImP), improve reliability of interpretation.

Interpretation centers on identifying and quantifying three major phenomena: penetration/aspiration, pharyngeal transit and clearance, and structural/mechanical impairment.

- Penetration and aspiration: Penetration refers to entry of material into the laryngeal vestibule above the level of the vocal folds; aspiration is passage of material below the vocal folds into the trachea. The Penetration-Aspiration Scale (PAS) is commonly used; it is an 8-point ordinal scale where 1 indicates no material enters the airway and 8 indicates material enters the airway, passes below the vocal folds, and no effort is made to eject. Silent aspiration is defined by lack of cough or throat-clear response despite material entering the airway. VFSS allows determination of when aspiration occurs (pre-, intra-, or post-swallow), which is crucial for mechanistic interpretation and management.

- Pharyngeal transit and clearance: Normal pharyngeal transit is rapid, with pharyngeal phase typically lasting less than 1 second for a single trial of thin liquids. VFSS evaluates bolus propulsion from the valleculae and pyriform sinuses, pharyngeal stripping wave (pharyngeal constriction), tongue base retraction, and the degree of bolus residue. Quantification of residue can be subjective, but scales and pixel-based measures exist. Significant residue in the valleculae or pyriform sinuses increases the risk of post-swallow aspiration.

- Upper esophageal sphincter (UES) opening: VFSS demonstrates the degree and duration of UES opening, which depends on hyolaryngeal excursion and cricopharyngeal relaxation. Reduced UES opening results in bolus stasis at the level of the pyriform sinuses and may lead to post-swallow aspiration. VFSS also allows visualization of pharyngeal diverticula, Zenker’s diverticulum, and overt structural lesions.

Interpreting VFSS requires integrating observations: delayed swallow trigger with preserved pharyngeal contraction points to sensory impairment; poor tongue base retraction and pharyngeal constriction imply weakness of pharyngeal musculature; incomplete laryngeal elevation with early airway invasion indicates risk during the pharyngeal phase. Compensatory strategies (e.g., chin-tuck, head turn) can be tested in real time to determine whether they reduce aspiration or improve bolus flow. The diagnostic output guides diet recommendations, rehabilitation strategies, and decisions about further investigations or interventions.

# FEES methodology and complementary role to videofluoroscopy

Fibreoptic endoscopic evaluation of swallowing (FEES) is an instrumental assessment performed at bedside or clinic that uses a flexible nasendoscope to visualize pharyngeal and laryngeal structures before and after the swallow. FEES provides high-resolution views of mucosal anatomy, secretions, pooling, residue, and laryngeal penetration/aspiration. It is particularly valuable for repeated assessments, biofeedback during therapy, and evaluation of secretion management.

Methodology: FEES is performed by passing a transnasal flexible endoscope to a position above the epiglottis and close to the tip of the epiglottis or in the hypopharynx, allowing visualization of the velopharyngeal port, pharyngeal walls, laryngeal vestibule, and vocal folds. Topical nasal anesthesia may be used sparingly. The patient is given food and liquid boluses of varying consistencies mixed with food coloring (commonly blue or green) to improve contrast. The clinician observes baseline anatomy, secretions and pooling, pharyngeal squeeze on phonation, swallow initiation (white-out due to epiglottic inversion occurs during the swallow), and post-swallow residue and penetration/aspiration events.

Strengths and limitations: FEES excels at identifying structural abnormalities, mucosal lesions, and secretion management issues. It detects aspiration and penetration when occurring before and after the swallow and is well-suited for patients who cannot be transferred to radiology or are medically unstable. FEES allows prolonged observation and repeated trials, enabling assessment of fatigue effects and response to therapeutic maneuvers, making it ideal for real-time treatment trials and biofeedback.

FEES limitations include inability to directly visualize the pharyngeal and laryngeal structures during the actual swallow due to the brief "white-out" period when the pharynx closes around the endoscope. Therefore, aspiration that occurs strictly during the swallow (intra-swallow) can be inferred only by pre- and post-swallow findings and clinical correlation. FEES also gives no information on oral transit or UES opening and cannot assess esophageal clearance beyond the immediate post-swallow. Additionally, a small percentage of patients cannot tolerate nasal endoscopy.

Complementary roles: VFSS and FEES are complementary rather than competing modalities. VFSS provides dynamic visualization of oral and pharyngeal phases including UES opening and direct assessment during the swallow, which is essential for identifying timing of aspiration and bolus transit. FEES provides superior mucosal detail, secretion assessment, and detailed observation of pharyngeal residue and airway protective reflexes. Use FEES for bedside, serial monitoring, and therapeutic biofeedback; use VFSS when assessment of oral phase, transit times, UES function, or suspected structural abnormalities above the UES are required. Many centers employ both: FEES for initial bedside screening and serial therapy, VFSS for comprehensive physiologic analysis and surgical planning.

# Clinical bedside swallowing assessments and aspiration risk stratification

Bedside clinical assessment is the initial step in evaluating swallowing function and aspiration risk. It informs decisions about immediate management and need for instrumental testing. A thorough bedside assessment integrates history, cranial nerve and oromotor examination, observation of speech and secretions, simple swallowing trials, and validated screening tools.

History should elicit onset, progression, food consistencies provoking symptoms, associated symptoms (coughing, choking, voice change, drooling, nasal regurgitation), prior strokes or neurologic disease, and ability to manage secretions. Cognitive status and level of alertness are crucial since attention and cooperation affect swallowing safety.

Oromotor examination evaluates lip closure, lingual strength and range, palatal elevation, gag reflex (limited sensitivity), symmetry of facial movements, and voluntary cough strength. Assessing respiratory function and cough is essential because an effective cough protects the airway; peak cough flow below certain thresholds correlates with aspiration risk (e.g., peak cough flow < 160 L/min has been associated with poor airway clearance). Voice quality is assessed for wet/gurgly quality, hoarseness, or breathiness, which may indicate laryngeal dysfunction or aspiration.

Simple swallowing trials begin with small amounts of water or other liquids and progress through thicker consistencies and solids if safe. The 3-ounce water swallow challenge is a commonly used screening test: patients drink 90 mL (3 ounces) continuously from a cup. Failure is any coughing, throat clearing, or voice change during or immediately after drinking. The test has reasonable sensitivity but limited specificity, and positive findings should prompt instrumental assessment. Other validated bedside tools include the Gugging Swallowing Screen (GUSS) and the Mann Assessment of Swallowing Ability (MASA), which stratify risk and recommend diet modifications.

Aspiration risk stratification considers multiple domains: alertness and cognition, oromotor function, respiratory status, voluntary cough, presence of secretions, voice quality, and performance on water swallow trials. High-risk features include reduced consciousness, inability to manage secretions, absent or severely weak cough, wet voice after swallowing, and documented or suspected silent aspiration. In such patients, oral intake should be suspended until instrumental evaluation clarifies safety. Intermediate risk patients with mild cough or voice change may be allowed limited oral trials with supervised therapy and expedited instrumental testing. Low-risk patients with normal oromotor function and negative screening tests may begin graded diet advancement.

Clinical bedside assessment has limitations: it cannot reliably detect silent aspiration and may underestimate pharyngeal residue or UES dysfunction. Therefore, any uncertainty or high pre-test probability of aspiration mandates instrumental evaluation with VFSS or FEES. Documenting the details of bedside tests and clear rationale for subsequent steps is important for continuity of care and medico-legal reasons.

# Management principles: dietary modification, rehabilitative therapy, and indications for enteral feeding

Management of neurogenic oropharyngeal dysphagia aims to prevent aspiration and malnutrition, optimize safe oral intake, and rehabilitate swallowing function where possible. Strategies include compensatory measures and diet modification, rehabilitative exercises and maneuvers, medical and surgical interventions in selected cases, and enteral feeding for severe or refractory dysphagia.

Dietary modification and compensatory strategies form the first-line approach. Modifying bolus volume and consistency (thickened liquids, pureed solids) can reduce aspiration risk by improving swallow timing and bolus control. Texture classification systems (e.g., International Dysphagia Diet Standardisation Initiative, IDDSI) recommend specific levels; clinicians should ensure recommendations align with local dietetics. Postural adjustments (chin-tuck/neck flexion, head-turn/head rotation toward a weaker side, chin-up for improved oral bolus transfer) alter oropharyngeal geometry to reduce aspiration risk and can be tested during VFSS or FEES for efficacy. Maneuvers such as the supraglottic or super-supraglottic swallow, effortful swallow, Mendelsohn maneuver, and tongue-hold (Masako) target specific physiologic deficits: supraglottic maneuvers enhance airway protection during swallow; effortful swallows increase pharyngeal pressure generation; Mendelsohn prolongs hyolaryngeal elevation and UES opening. The choice of strategy is individualized based on instrumental assessment findings.

Rehabilitative therapy encompasses exercises to strengthen impaired muscles, improve coordination, and retrain sensory awareness. Progressive resistance exercises (isometric/isokinetic exercises for suprahyoid and tongue muscles), neuromuscular electrical stimulation (NMES) in select settings, and sensory enhancement techniques (thermal-tactile stimulation) are often used. Evidence supports targeted exercise programs—e.g., tongue resistance training, Shaker head lift exercise to improve UES opening, and expiratory muscle strength training to augment cough strength and airway clearance—in improving physiologic measures and functional outcomes for some patients. Neuroplasticity principles guide therapy intensity and frequency: repetitive, task-specific practice yields better outcomes. In progressive neurologic conditions, rehabilitative therapy focuses both on maintenance of function and safe swallowing strategies as the disease advances.

Medical and surgical options are considered for refractory or specific anatomic/physiologic deficits. Botulinum toxin injection into the cricopharyngeus can be effective for cricopharyngeal dysfunction with impaired UES relaxation. Cricopharyngeal myotomy is a surgical option for select patients with structural or neuromuscular dysfunction of the UES when conservative measures fail and the patient’s overall condition supports surgery. Laryngeal elevation procedures, vocal fold medialization (for unilateral vocal fold paralysis causing aspiration), and other targeted interventions are reserved for carefully selected cases.

Indications for enteral feeding (nasoenteric tube or percutaneous endoscopic gastrostomy, PEG) include inability to protect the airway from aspiration despite compensatory measures, inability to meet nutritional and hydration needs by oral intake, anticipated prolonged dysphagia with poor swallowing recovery (e.g., persistent severe dysphagia after large brainstem stroke), and recurrent aspiration pneumonia despite appropriate therapy. Decision-making must balance risks and benefits, taking into account prognosis, patient preferences, cognitive status, and goals of care. Short-term nasogastric tubes are appropriate for temporary support; PEG placement is considered when long-term enteral feeding is anticipated (typically beyond 4–6 weeks) and the patient’s condition and prognosis justify the procedure. Enteral feeding reduces the risk of malnutrition and dehydration but does not eliminate pulmonary aspiration risk because aspiration of oropharyngeal secretions can still occur; therefore, aspiration precautions and continued swallowing therapy remain relevant after tube placement.

Nutritional assessment and monitoring are integral. Baseline weight, body mass index, serum albumin and prealbumin can guide nutritional status assessment, with recognition that serum proteins are influenced by inflammation and not solely by nutrition. Target caloric and protein needs depend on medical condition; dietitian involvement is essential for tailored plans and monitoring.

Finally, clinical care must integrate multidisciplinary collaboration: neurologists, speech-language pathologists, dietitians, otolaryngologists, gastroenterologists, and nursing staff coordinate to assess risk, perform instrumental evaluations, implement therapy, and make shared decisions regarding feeding routes. Regular reassessment is important because swallowing function can change over time with recovery, progression, or response to therapy.

# Summary

Neurogenic oropharyngeal dysphagia arises from diverse lesions affecting cortical, subcortical, brainstem, cranial nerve, neuromuscular junction, and muscle targets. Distinguishing oropharyngeal from esophageal dysphagia is primarily a clinical task, but instrumental studies are often required. Videofluoroscopy provides dynamic assessment of oral and pharyngeal physiology and is the gold standard for evaluating aspiration, penetration, transit, and UES function. FEES offers complementary strengths in bedside applicability, mucosal visualization, and repeated therapeutic trials. Bedside screening and clinical assessment stratify aspiration risk and guide urgency of instrumental studies. Management is multidisciplinary and tailored: compensatory diet and postural strategies, rehabilitative exercises targeting specific physiologic deficits, selective medical or surgical interventions, and enteral feeding when aspiration risk or nutritional compromise cannot be managed orally. Understanding the physiologic basis of observed swallowing deficits enables rational selection of diagnostic tests and individualized therapeutic strategies.

Introduction

Bulbar dysfunction and choking are clinical syndromes encountered across multiple specialties, including neurology, otolaryngology (ENT), gastroenterology, and primary care. “Bulbar” refers to involvement of cranial nerve nuclei in the medulla (the bulbar region), their lower motor neuron axons, or the muscles they innervate, producing dysarthria, dysphonia, dysphagia, chewing difficulty, and impaired cough or airway protection. Choking episodes may reflect transient aspiration, airway obstruction, impaired swallowing coordination, or heightened sensation/behavioral phenomena. The differential diagnosis is broad and spans neuromuscular junction disorders, motor neuron and other neurodegenerative diseases, vascular and inflammatory lesions of the brainstem, structural and mucosal diseases of the oropharynx and larynx, gastroesophageal reflux-related laryngopharyngeal disease, and psychogenic/functional disorders. A systematic approach integrating history, examination, bedside tests, directed ancillary testing, and multidisciplinary evaluation maximizes diagnostic yield while avoiding unnecessary studies or delays in treatment.

This chapter organizes the clinical reasoning necessary to sort these causes, emphasizing pathophysiology, key features that distinguish entities, high-yield investigations, and red flags that prompt urgent intervention.

# Neuromuscular junction disorders: myasthenia gravis evaluation and red flags

Neuromuscular junction (NMJ) disorders produce fatigable weakness of voluntary muscles. Myasthenia gravis (MG) is the prototypical autoimmune NMJ disorder, caused by antibodies targeting postsynaptic acetylcholine receptors (AChR) or associated proteins such as muscle-specific kinase (MuSK) and low-density lipoprotein receptor-related protein 4 (LRP4). The pathophysiology involves complement-mediated receptor loss, impaired neuromuscular transmission, and reduced safety factor for neuromuscular transmission. Bulbar muscles are commonly involved because their high-frequency discharge and fine control make them susceptible to impaired transmission; hence MG commonly causes diplopia, ptosis, dysarthria, dysphagia, and fatigable chewing.

Clinical evaluation emphasizes fatigability and fluctuation: symptoms worsen with exertion and improve with rest. In bulbar MG, the hallmark is progressive dysarthria or dysphagia that fluctuates through the day and responds to rest or neostigmine/edrophonium challenge. Examination may reveal fatigable ptosis, gaze-limited ophthalmoparesis, nasal dysarthria, reduced palatal elevation, and weakness of neck extensors. Respiratory muscle weakness manifests as reduced vital capacity and may progress to myasthenic crisis.

Diagnostic testing proceeds in stages. Serum antibodies are first-line: AChR binding antibodies are positive in approximately 85–90% of generalized MG and in a lower proportion of purely ocular MG; MuSK antibodies are more common in seronegative generalized MG and often associate with prominent bulbar and respiratory involvement. LRP4 antibodies are less frequent but present in some seronegative patients. Reference interpretation: a negative antibody panel does not exclude MG.

Electrophysiology is essential when antibody testing is negative or to confirm diagnosis. Repetitive nerve stimulation (RNS) at 2–3 Hz shows a decremental response in postsynaptic disorders. Single-fiber electromyography (SFEMG) is the most sensitive test for impaired neuromuscular transmission, detecting increased jitter and blocking even when RNS is normal. Pharmacologic testing historically used edrophonium (Tensilon) for transient improvement; edrophonium is less commonly used now because of false positives and cholinergic risks, but neostigmine or an oral pyridostigmine trial may also support the diagnosis clinically.

Red flags in the setting of suspected MG include rapidly progressive bulbar symptoms, hypoxia, stridor, ineffective cough, inability to handle secretions, and vital capacity falling below 20 mL/kg or dropping rapidly—these require urgent monitoring and consideration for respiratory support. Bulbar-dominant MG with severe dysphagia can lead to aspiration pneumonia and malnutrition, mandating multidisciplinary management with speech therapy and nutritional assessment.

Laboratory and ancillary studies supportive of evaluation include routine tests to exclude mimics: creatine kinase (CK) is typically normal or only mildly elevated in MG; markedly elevated CK suggests myopathy rather than NMJ disorder (normal CK varies by lab, commonly approximately 30–200 U/L). Chest imaging (contrast-enhanced CT or MRI) is indicated to evaluate for thymoma in AChR antibody–positive disease because thymoma has implications for oncologic resection and immunotherapy.

Treatment decisions hinge on confirming the diagnosis and assessing severity. Rapidly progressive bulbar weakness or respiratory compromise should prompt hospital admission, pulmonary function monitoring, and early involvement of neurology and critical care. Immunotherapies, cholinesterase inhibitors, thymectomy for thymoma, and supportive measures are guided by disease classification and severity.

# Motor neuron diseases (bulbar-onset ALS) and other neurodegenerative causes

Motor neuron disease (MND), particularly amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS), is a key neurodegenerative cause of progressive bulbar dysfunction. In ALS, there is degeneration of both upper motor neurons (UMN) in the cortex and lower motor neurons (LMN) in the brainstem and spinal cord. Bulbar-onset ALS presents with progressive dysarthria, dysphagia, tongue wasting and fasciculations, and sialorrhea. Distinguishing features include a combination of LMN signs in bulbar-innervated muscles (atrophy, fasciculations, reduced reflexes) together with UMN signs (spasticity, hyperreflexia, pathological reflexes) either locally or in other body regions. Bulbar-onset disease tends to have faster progression and worse prognosis compared with limb-onset ALS; aspiration and respiratory failure are common causes of morbidity.

Diagnostic criteria for ALS have been formalized (revised El Escorial and Awaji criteria), emphasizing the combination of LMN signs in multiple regions and UMN signs, progression over time, and exclusion of mimics. Electromyography (EMG) demonstrates widespread denervation and reinnervation in multiple segments, including evidence of active denervation (fibrillation potentials, positive sharp waves) and chronic neurogenic changes such as large motor unit potentials. For bulbar-onset presentations, EMG of tongue and limb muscles is helpful. Laboratory tests and imaging are required to exclude treatable mimics (metabolic, structural, inflammatory, infectious) that can produce UMN/LMN features.

Other neurodegenerative disorders can produce bulbar symptoms. Progressive supranuclear palsy and multiple system atrophy can have dysphagia and dysarthria as part of a broader syndrome with ocular or autonomic features. Spinocerebellar ataxias, late-onset Tay–Sachs variants, and inclusion-body myositis may mimic bulbar weakness. In inclusion-body myositis, dysphagia occurs due to pharyngeal muscle involvement; CK may be mildly to moderately elevated and EMG/myopathology will show myopathic changes with rimmed vacuoles. Differentiation relies on pattern recognition, labs (e.g., CK), EMG, muscle biopsy when indicated, and MRI of brain and spinal cord.

Prognostication and urgent decision points: progressive weight loss, frequent aspiration pneumonia, and shortening of intervals between choking/aspiration events signal need for nutritional and respiratory interventions. Measurement of forced vital capacity (FVC) and sniff nasal inspiratory pressure are key for monitoring respiratory involvement; FVC < 50% predicted or rapid decline are indications for noninvasive ventilation referral and advanced care planning discussions. Referral to multidisciplinary ALS clinics improves outcomes.

# Brainstem stroke, demyelinating disease and compressive lesions

Focal lesions of the brainstem can produce acute or subacute bulbar dysfunction. Vascular events such as lateral medullary (Wallenberg) infarcts classically cause bulbar dysfunction with dysphagia, hoarseness (nucleus ambiguus involvement), ipsilateral facial sensory loss, and contralateral body pain/temp sensory loss. Paramedian pontine lesions can affect corticobulbar fibers producing dysarthria and impairment of bulbar motor control. Strokes are usually abrupt in onset and accompanied by other focal signs—vertigo, ataxia, hemiparesis, sensory changes, ocular motor palsies—depending on vascular territory.

Demyelinating disease such as multiple sclerosis (MS) can involve the brainstem producing internuclear ophthalmoplegia, facial numbness or weakness, and acute or subacute bulbar signs. In MS, lesions often disseminate in time and space and are accompanied by typical MRI white-matter lesions and oligoclonal bands in cerebrospinal fluid. Brainstem demyelination may present with diplopia and dysarthria in young adults, raising suspicion for MS when clinical context and MRI findings fit.

Compressive lesions of the posterior fossa and foramen magnum—tumors (e.g., metastases, primary gliomas, meningiomas), Chiari malformation, vertebral artery dissection with mass effect, and infectious/inflammatory processes—can produce progressive bulbar signs. Compression may result in isolated lower cranial nerve palsies, progression over weeks to months, and signs of increased intracranial pressure. Skull base lesions (jugular foramen syndromes) produce specific patterns involving CNs IX–XI.

Diagnostic approach to suspected central causes prioritizes neuroimaging. MRI of the brain with diffusion-weighted imaging (DWI) is essential to detect acute ischemia; MRI brainstem sequences and high-resolution T2/FLAIR sequences increase sensitivity for demyelinating plaques and small structural lesions. When vascular dissection or posterior circulation pathology is suspected, MR or CT angiography is indicated. Lumbar puncture is considered when inflammatory or infectious processes are in the differential. Urgent neurovascular coordination is necessary for acute ischemic stroke; swallowing assessment and aspiration precautions are critical because acute bulbar dysfunction markedly increases aspiration risk.

Key clinical decision points: an abrupt onset of bulbar symptoms with focal neurological deficits mandates emergent MRI/DWI and neurovascular evaluation; subacute or progressive focal deficits require MRI with contrast to evaluate for neoplasm or demyelination, and contrast-enhanced imaging of the skull base for suspected compressive lesions. Early ENT involvement for airway assessment is indicated when laryngeal dysfunction or airway compromise is suspected.

# Structural lesions of the oropharynx and larynx, and reflux-related laryngopharyngeal disease

Anatomic and mucosal pathology of the oropharynx and larynx is a common non-neurologic cause of choking, cough, hoarseness, and dysphagia. Structural lesions include tumors (squamous cell carcinoma of the oropharynx/larynx, hypopharyngeal carcinoma), infectious processes (abscesses, epiglottitis), iatrogenic or traumatic injuries, foreign bodies, vocal cord paralysis, and age-related changes (presbyphagia). Pathophysiology ranges from mechanical obstruction of the airway or bolus pathway, impaired epiglottic or laryngeal elevation, to vocal fold immobility causing ineffective airway protection and aspiration.

Endoscopic evaluation is key: flexible nasoendoscopy with stroboscopy performed by ENT can visualize vocal fold mobility, structural lesions, mucosal abnormalities, and secretion pooling. Vocal fold paralysis is often unilateral and causes breathy dysphonia and aspiration with liquids; bilateral vocal fold paralysis can present with stridor and life-threatening airway compromise. The presence of a mass, mucosal irregularity, asymmetric movement, or cricopharyngeal dysfunction on endoscopy necessitates imaging (contrast CT or MRI of the neck) and tissue diagnosis via biopsy for suspected malignancy.

Laryngopharyngeal reflux (LPR) produces chronic laryngeal symptoms without classic heartburn and is implicated in chronic cough, throat clearing, hoarseness, globus sensation, and recurrent aspiration-like episodes. Pathophysiology involves episodic reflux of gastric contents (acid and non-acid) that cause inflammation of the laryngeal and pharyngeal mucosa, impairing laryngeal sensation and coordination of swallowing. Diagnosis is clinical and supported by ENT laryngoscopic findings (erythema, posterior laryngeal edema, interarytenoid hypertrophy), but these signs are nonspecific. Objective testing using 24-hour pH impedance monitoring can confirm pathological acid or non-acid reflux episodes reaching the hypopharynx. Empiric trials of proton pump inhibitors (PPI) are frequently used, but randomized data are mixed and PPIs may not alter non-acid reflux. Lifestyle modification and speech–language therapy for voice and reflux behavioral modification are also therapeutic pillars.

Barium swallow or videofluoroscopic swallowing study (VFSS) assesses oropharyngeal and esophageal phases, revealing aspiration patterns, pooling, penetration, cricopharyngeal dysfunction, and structural obstruction. Esophagogastroduodenoscopy (EGD) evaluates for esophageal strictures, rings, webs, or malignancy that may produce choking or regurgitation. Decision-making about surgical intervention (tumor resection, laryngeal framework surgery, cricopharyngeal myotomy) requires correlation between symptoms, endoscopy, imaging, and swallow study findings.

Key clinical thresholds include urgent ENT evaluation for stridor, drooling, impending airway obstruction, or signs of upper airway infection/abscess. Any unexplained persistent hoarseness > 2–4 weeks, especially with risk factors for malignancy (smoking, alcohol), warrants laryngoscopic evaluation and possible biopsy.

# Psychogenic and functional causes of choking and cough

Psychogenic (functional) causes can mimic bulbar dysfunction or produce paroxysmal choking and cough in the absence of structural or neurophysiologic disease. Functional dysphagia and functional cough (formerly psychogenic cough or somatic cough disorder) are manifestations of altered sensorimotor control of the larynx and pharynx, often with heightened perception, maladaptive behaviors, and central processing differences. Conversion disorder may present with dysphagia, globus sensation, or episodic choking that is inconsistent with organic disease.

Clinical features suggesting a functional disorder include abrupt onset temporally related to stressors, variability and distractibility of symptoms, inconsistency between reported severity and objective findings, normal structural and electrophysiologic testing, and symptom improvement with suggestion or distraction. In functional cough, cough may be absent during sleep and triggered by attention or suggestion; in functional dysphagia, instrumental swallowing studies may show inconsistent bolus flow, normal anatomy, and preserved coordination during nonprovoked tasks.

Diagnosis is one of positive identification rather than exclusion alone: recognition of typical features, normal investigations that reliably exclude structural and neurologic causes, and the presence of psychological comorbidity support a functional diagnosis. However, because organic causes can coexist or be intermittent, careful and methodical evaluation is essential before assigning a psychogenic label. Multidisciplinary management includes speech–language therapy with behavioral cough suppression and retraining, cognitive behavioral therapy, treatment of coexisting anxiety/depression, and reassurance. Importantly, avoid dismissive attitudes; patients benefit from a clear explanation that their symptoms are real and potentially reversible with targeted therapies.

# Diagnostic algorithms and prioritized testing strategy (ENT, GI, neurology, imaging)

A pragmatic diagnostic algorithm begins with a focused history and targeted physical examination that clarifies three key questions: (1) Is there evidence of acute neurological injury requiring emergent management? (2) Are signs of airway compromise or aspiration present requiring immediate protective measures? (3) Is there a likely structural or mucosal ENT/GI cause versus a neuromuscular or central neurological cause?

Initial triage: any patient with stridor, hypoxia, inability to handle secretions, progressive dysphagia with weight loss, or signs of acute focal neurologic deficit should receive immediate airway stabilization and urgent imaging (MRI brain with DWI for suspected stroke, CT neck for airway compromise) and specialty consultation (ENT, neurology, critical care).

Baseline studies appropriate for most patients include pulse oximetry, bedside swallowing assessment (e.g., water swallow test), speech–language pathology evaluation, and nasoendoscopy if ENT symptoms predominate. Bedside tests that suggest neuromuscular transmission failure include fatigability maneuvers (sustained upgaze, repetitive speech) and assessment for ptosis or ocular motility abnormalities.

When ENT structural disease is suspected due to hoarseness, stridor, visible mass, or focal laryngeal symptoms, flexible laryngoscopy is first-line. Findings of vocal fold immobility, mucosal lesions, or pooling of secretions prompt cross-sectional imaging of the neck (contrast CT or MRI) and biopsy for suspected neoplasm. If oropharyngeal or esophageal obstruction is suspected, VFSS (barium swallow) and/or EGD are indicated. If reflux is suspected, a trial of behavioral therapy and medical treatment may be attempted—but in refractory or atypical cases, 24-hour pH impedance monitoring is the definitive test for objective reflux reaching the hypopharynx.

When a neuromuscular junction disorder is suspected (fluctuating fatigable weakness, ocular involvement, preserved reflexes early), obtain serologic testing for AChR and MuSK antibodies and refer for EMG/SFEMG. Chest CT to assess for thymoma should accompany positive AChR antibody results. In suspected generalized MG with respiratory compromise, hospital admission and pulmonary function testing are urgent.

When motor neuron disease or other central neurodegenerative process is suspected (progressive course, LMN/UMN signs, tongue fasciculations), perform EMG demonstrating denervation in multiple segments and MRI to exclude structural lesions. Routine labs to exclude metabolic mimics (thyroid function tests, B12, infectious serologies) should be included. Muscle enzyme testing (CK) and, when appropriate, muscle biopsy can distinguish myopathies such as inclusion-body myositis.

If acute onset with focal brainstem signs, MRI brain with DWI is the priority to detect ischemia; vascular imaging (CTA or MRA) follows for posterior circulation evaluation. When demyelinating disease is suspected, MRI brain and spine with contrast and lumbar puncture for CSF analysis including oligoclonal bands are appropriate.

A prioritized, time-sensitive testing flow might be conceptualized as follows: immediate bedside airway and swallowing assessment; if alarm features present, urgent imaging and specialty consultation; if not, use history/exam to steer early testing: ENT (flexible laryngoscopy, VFSS) for suspected structural/laryngeal disease; neurology (antibody testing, EMG, MRI brainstem) for suspected NMJ, MND, or central causes; GI (EGD, pH impedance) for suspected reflux/esophageal causes. Throughout, coordinate multidisciplinary input—ENT for laryngeal visualization and airway management, speech-language pathologists for swallowing assessment and therapy, neurologists for neuromuscular evaluation, gastroenterologists for esophageal and reflux assessment, and radiologists for directed imaging.

Key diagnostic thresholds and normal ranges to remember: serum AChR and MuSK antibody assays are qualitative but positive results are diagnostic when clinical context aligns; normal CK is commonly approximately 30–200 U/L (lab dependent), and markedly elevated CK suggests primary myopathy rather than NMJ or ALS. Pulmonary function thresholds guiding urgency include a decline in forced vital capacity to <50% predicted or absolute values that fall rapidly or approach 20 mL/kg for consideration of ventilatory support. VFSS findings of aspiration on thin liquids require urgent dietary modification and swallowing therapy. Positive pH impedance monitoring demonstrating increased acid exposure time or proximal reflux episodes supports LPR diagnosis when correlated with symptoms.

Conclusions

Bulbar dysfunction and choking demand a broad, integrated approach. Recognizing patterns—fluctuating fatigability for NMJ disorders, progressive combined UMN/LMN signs for motor neuron disease, abrupt focal deficits for vascular lesions, structural or mucosal laryngeal findings for ENT pathology, and characteristic features of functional disorders—guides efficient investigation. Multidisciplinary assessment with timely use of endoscopy, imaging, electrophysiology, swallow studies, and reflux testing yields accurate diagnoses and expedites appropriate therapies. Urgent referral and airway protection remain paramount when red flags are present.

Chapter 14: Neuropathology of Brainstem Compression and Syrinx Formation

Introduction

Chronic compression of the brainstem and the formation of intramedullary cavities (syringomyelia) are important causes of progressive neurological dysfunction. The neuropathology of these processes reflects a spectrum of mechanical, vascular, and secondary degenerative changes that evolve over time and determine clinical presentation and outcome. Understanding the cascade from initial mechanical deformation through ischemia, ependymal breach, gliosis, demyelination, and neuronal loss is essential for interpreting clinical signs, guiding imaging interpretation, anticipating complications, and recognizing factors that influence recovery after decompression. This chapter reviews the cellular and structural changes that occur with protracted brainstem compression and syrinx formation, addresses current models of syringogenesis emphasizing fluid dynamics and perivascular flow, describes the characteristic microscopic pathology, links anatomical injury to respiratory, cranial nerve, and spinal cord deficits, discusses the extent to which changes are reversible after relief of compression, and summarizes insights gained from animal models.

# Ischemic and degenerative changes from chronic compression

Chronic mechanical compression subjects the brainstem to sustained deformation and altered microvascular perfusion. The brainstem's dense concentration of vital nuclei and long tracts means that even modest, persistent pressure can produce significant dysfunction. Pathophysiologically, compression raises interstitial and intravascular pressures locally and disrupts autoregulatory mechanisms, leading to a combination of reduced capillary perfusion, venous congestion, and ischemia-reperfusion injury over time.

Early stages are often dominated by focal ischemia driven by arteriolar narrowing and compromised capillary flow. Unlike large-artery occlusion, compression produces a gradient of ischemia: subependymal and perivascular regions, watershed regions between vascular territories, and regions with relatively tenuous collateralization are most vulnerable. Neurons are highly susceptible to hypoxia; within minutes they manifest reversible changes such as dendritic beading and cytoplasmic eosinophilia, whereas irreversible neuronal loss develops over hours to days if perfusion is not restored. In the chronically compressed brainstem, repeated episodes of low-grade ischemia and intermittent hypoperfusion promote cumulative neuronal and axonal injury.

Venous outflow obstruction is a key contributor. Compression impairs small venous drainage and leads to increased interstitial fluid, tissue edema, and elevated tissue pressure that further diminishes capillary perfusion. Chronic venous hypertension also predisposes to blood–brain barrier disruption and capillary leakage. These processes encourage a progressive cycle: edema increases tissue pressure, reduces perfusion, worsens hypoxia, and incites further edema.

Degenerative changes in white matter are pronounced. Axonal transport is mechanically and metabolically compromised, leading to axonal swelling, fragmentation, and eventual Wallerian-type degeneration both anterograde and retrograde relative to the site of compression. Myelin sheaths disintegrate secondarily to axonal loss and to direct oligodendrocyte injury from ischemia. Chronic compression also induces microglial and astrocytic activation, driving phagocytosis of myelin and cellular debris and establishing a gliotic scar that replaces lost parenchyma.

Chronic deformation also stimulates extracellular matrix remodeling and fibrosis, particularly in subpial and subependymal zones where meningeal and ependymal interfaces experience shear stress. This remodeling can stiffen the region and perpetuate focal compression even after partial surgical decompression, contributing to persistent deficits.

Clinically, the interplay of ischemia and degeneration explains progressive deficits that may worsen insidiously. Signs reflecting neurons with high metabolic demand or long axons (e.g., bulbar nuclei, corticospinal tracts) often manifest early. The timescale of degeneration is variable and influenced by degree and intermittency of compression, vascular reserve, and individual tissue susceptibility.

# Pathophysiology of syrinx formation: fluid dynamics, perivascular flow and ependymal injury

Syringomyelia—formation of an intramedullary fluid-filled cavity (syrinx)—has complex and evolving pathogenetic hypotheses. Contemporary understanding integrates altered cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) dynamics, transmission of pulsatile pressures into the cord, disruption of ependymal integrity, and interstitial and perivascular fluid flow.

A cardinal initiating factor is abnormal CSF flow in the subarachnoid space around the foramen magnum or spinal canal. Chiari malformations, arachnoid scarring, tumors, and post-inflammatory adhesions can produce focal obstruction or impedance to normal craniospinal CSF oscillations. With each cardiac cycle, arterial expansion transmits pulsations to the CSF; when outflow is impeded, pressure waves are amplified or redistributed. Two broad mechanisms are emphasized:

1) Pulsation-driven transmission into the cord: Elevated subarachnoid pressure waves may be transmitted into the spinal cord parenchyma via perivascular (Virchow-Robin) spaces surrounding penetrating arteries. These spaces serve as conduits for fluid oscillations; forced transmission of augmented pulses can drive interstitial fluid movement centrally, progressively distending the parenchyma and coalescing into a cavity.

2) One-way valve or piston mechanism: Dynamic crowding at the foramen magnum or focal arachnoid narrowings may produce a piston-like effect, where downward movement of the cerebellar tonsils during systole forces CSF into spinal subarachnoid spaces but prevents its return during diastole. This cyclic pressure differential can drive fluid into the cord. Similarly, spinal canal crowding during trauma or degenerative disease can cause segmental piston effects.

Ependymal injury is central to cavitation. The ependyma provides a barrier between CSF and the spinal cord parenchyma; mechanical stress, ischemia, inflammation, or direct shear forces can denude or disrupt the ependymal lining. Once breached, CSF or interstitial fluid gains access to the central canal or parenchymal extracellular space, allowing small foci of edema to expand. Progressive dissolution or coalescence of microcystic spaces forming around necrotic or degenerated tissue creates a macroscopic syrinx.

Perivascular flow plays a key role in maintenance and expansion of a syrinx. Experimental and imaging studies suggest that fluid can track along perivascular channels from the subarachnoid space into the cord. Aquaporin channels (notably aquaporin-4 on astrocytic endfeet) modulate water movement in the interstitial space; altered expression or dysfunction of these channels in response to chronic stress may change water handling and favor cavity formation.

Hydrodynamic considerations also explain directional growth of a syrinx. Pressure gradients, tissue compliance, and tethering determine whether a cavity expands longitudinally (along the cord), centrally (toward the central canal), or laterally (toward dorsal or ventral horns). Tethering at the foramen magnum or adhesions can focus distention in particular segments. Additionally, repetitive micro-injuries from pulsatile flow and shear stress can cause progressive tissue breakdown at the cavity margins.

Inflammation contributes as well. Chemical mediators released from injured tissue and infiltrating inflammatory cells can increase vascular permeability and promote degeneration. However, syrinx fluid is usually largely acellular and similar to CSF chemistry, which suggests that pure inflammatory exudation is not the sole mechanism.

In summary, syrinx formation is best conceptualized as a dynamic process in which abnormal CSF pulsations and impeded subarachnoid flow transmit forces into the cord via perivascular routes and through damaged ependyma, leading to interstitial fluid accumulation, tissue necrosis, and cavity coalescence.

# Microscopic features: gliosis, demyelination and neuronal loss in brainstem nuclei

Microscopically, chronic brainstem compression and syringomyelia share several pathological hallmarks: reactive gliosis, variable demyelination, axonal pathology, neuronal loss in selective nuclei, and changes in the ependyma and microvasculature.

Gliosis is the most consistent and prominent feature. Astrocytes proliferate and hypertrophy, expressing increased glial fibrillary acidic protein (GFAP), and form dense astrocytic scars that replace lost neuropil. In chronic cavities such as syringes, the cavity walls are often lined by reactive astrocytes and connective tissue rather than intact ependyma. This gliotic border can contain processes that bridge across the cavity and is associated with deposition of extracellular matrix proteins, including collagen and laminin, particularly when meningeal fibrosis or chronic inflammation is present.

Demyelination is prominent in regions subject to axonal degeneration or ischemia. Oligodendrocytes are susceptible to hypoxic injury; in chronic compression zones there is a loss of mature oligodendrocytes and marked reduction of myelin basic protein (MBP) staining. Myelin loss may be diffuse in white matter tracts near a syrinx or focal around areas of necrosis. Secondary demyelination often follows axonal transection, seen as Wallerian degeneration with accumulation of myelin debris and subsequent phagocytosis by microglia and macrophages.

Neuronal loss in brainstem nuclei is selective but clinically consequential. Motor nuclei (e.g., hypoglossal, nucleus ambiguus), respiratory centers in the ventrolateral medulla, and cranial nerve nuclei (III–XII depending on level of compression) can display shrunken, hypereosinophilic neurons or eventually be absent in regions of advanced atrophy. Neuronal loss is accompanied by microglial activation and often by myelin pallor in associated tracts.

Axonal pathology can be identified as spheroids—focal axonal swellings containing neurofilaments and organelles—on microscopy. Over time, these progress to fragmentation and clearance. The corticospinal tracts and long ascending sensory tracts show both primary axonal injury within compressed segments and trans-synaptic degeneration in connected regions.

Ependymal changes are central to syringomyelia pathology. The ependymal lining can display denudation, hypertrophy of residual cells, and focal accumulation of lysosomal material. In syrinx cavities, the ependymal lining is often discontinuous; where present it can show cuboidal to columnar cell morphology with variable ciliation, or a complete loss leading to astrogliotic cavity walls.

Microvascular changes include capillary rarefaction, basement membrane thickening, and perivascular fibrosis. Red blood cell extravasation or hemosiderin deposition may be present where repeated microhemorrhages have occurred. Chronic venous congestion can be evident as dilation of small veins and venules, and may be associated with hemosiderin-laden macrophages.

Immunohistochemical studies often reveal upregulation of markers for reactive astrocytes (GFAP), activated microglia (Iba1, CD68), and changes in water channel expression (aquaporin-4) at cavity margins. The pattern of these changes informs the chronicity: dense gliosis and extensive matrix deposition indicate a longstanding lesion, whereas more macrophage-rich histology with fresh axonal spheroids suggests an active, evolving process.

# Correlation of neuropathology with respiratory, cranial nerve and spinal cord signs

Clinical deficits map closely to the anatomic distribution of neuropathologic changes. Understanding the relationship between histopathology and functional anatomy enables clinicians to interpret presenting syndromes and predict progression.

Respiratory dysfunction arises from involvement of medullary respiratory centers and their afferent and efferent pathways. The pre-Bötzinger complex, dorsal respiratory group, and ventral respiratory group reside in the ventrolateral medulla and are metabolically active and highly interconnected. Neuronal loss or gliosis in these regions—through direct compression, ischemia, or syrinx extension—can impair rhythmogenesis and the integration of chemosensory inputs. Additionally, involvement of cranial nerve nuclei (e.g., nucleus ambiguus) compromises glossopharyngeal and vagal control of upper airway patency and laryngeal function, predisposing to aspiration and sleep-disordered breathing. Pathologically, even partial loss of these neuronal populations can generate clinically significant hypoventilation, abnormal respiratory patterning (e.g., ataxic breathing), or impaired cough reflex.

Cranial nerve signs reflect focal injury to the respective nuclei and exiting nerve roots within the brainstem. For instance, compression at the pontomedullary junction can produce facial weakness from facial nucleus involvement, abducens palsy from nucleus/ fascicle damage, and trigeminal sensory loss from trigeminal nucleus compromise. Pathological correlates include neuronal shrinkage or loss in the targeted nuclei, demyelination of cranial nerve fascicles, and gliotic scarring replacing damaged tissue. Lower cranial nerve dysfunction (IX–XII) often signals medullary involvement and may manifest as dysphagia, dysphonia, and impaired gag reflex, corresponding to neuronal loss in nucleus ambiguus and hypoglossal nucleus. Since the nuclei are small and functionally specialized, even localized microscopic damage produces distinct clinical syndromes.

Spinal cord signs in syringomyelia depend on both the cavity’s location and the degree of surrounding tract involvement. Central cavitation classically produces a "cape-like" distribution of dissociated sensory loss (pain and temperature) in the upper extremities because the crossing fibers of the spinothalamic tract in the anterior commissure are interrupted early. Microscopically, this corresponds to central cavitation with adjacent gliosis and degeneration of decussating spinothalamic fibers. As the syrinx expands dorsally or laterally, dorsal horn neurons and dorsal columns become affected, producing impairment of proprioception and vibration. Ventral expansion can damage corticospinal tracts, producing weakness and upper motor neuron signs below the lesion; pathological correlates are demyelination and axonal loss in the lateral corticospinal tracts. Motor neuron pools in the anterior horn may undergo degeneration if the cavity reaches the ventral horn, resulting in lower motor neuron signs at the level of the lesion.

Autonomic dysfunction—e.g., Horner’s syndrome or autonomic dysreflexia—arises from involvement of sympathetic pathways or interconnections in the intermediolateral cell columns (thoracic cord) and descending autonomic fibers. Microscopically, this corresponds to disruption of white matter tracts and neuronal loss in relevant autonomic centers.

Importantly, symptom patterns may evolve as syrinxes enlarge or as compressive lesions progress. Focal ischemic damage may initially cause transient dysfunction, whereas permanent neurological deficits are more likely when neuronal loss and gliotic scarring are established.

# Reversibility of changes after decompression and factors predicting recovery

The potential for neurological recovery after surgical decompression depends on the nature and chronicity of the underlying pathology. Reversible elements include edema, transient conduction block, and early demyelination where axons remain intact. Irreversible elements include frank neuronal loss, extensive axonal transection, and dense gliosis with matrix deposition.

At the cellular level, relief of compression often leads to reduction in interstitial edema and restoration of microvascular perfusion, which can rescue neurons and oligodendrocytes that have suffered sublethal injury. Early remyelination may occur if oligodendrocyte progenitor cells survive and axons remain structurally intact; this process can improve conduction over weeks to months. In syringomyelia, decompression that restores normal CSF flow may halt syrinx expansion and allow gradual collapse of the cavity. Following collapse, surrounding tissue can show partial reconstitution with decreased edema and reduced gliosis, and some degree of functional improvement is often seen.

However, established gliotic scars and loss of neurons limit recovery. Astrocytic scars replace lost neural elements and form physical and chemical barriers to axonal regeneration. Where corticospinal axons have been transected, functional recovery is typically poor because central axons regenerate very poorly in the adult CNS. Additionally, changes such as perivascular fibrosis and microvascular basement membrane thickening may persist, limiting full restoration of tissue homeostasis.

Predictors of recovery identified in clinical and pathological studies include:

- Duration of symptoms: Shorter intervals between symptom onset and decompression are strongly associated with greater recovery. Chronic deficits (months to years) are less likely to improve substantially.
- Severity and progression: Rapidly progressive or severe deficits—especially those associated with radiographic evidence of extensive cavitation or marked atrophy—carry a poorer prognosis.
- Imaging correlates: Smaller syrinx size, less spinal cord atrophy, and absence of extensive T2 hyperintense signal in adjacent cord tissue predict better outcomes. Signal changes that normalize after decompression correlate with reversible edema rather than permanent tissue loss.
- Age and comorbidities: Younger patients often have more plasticity and potential for remyelination; vascular comorbidities (e.g., diabetes, atherosclerosis) impair microvascular recovery and are associated with worse outcomes.
- Presence of residual mechanical factors: Persistent CSF flow obstruction, arachnoid scarring, or bony abnormalities that are not fully corrected increase the risk of recurrence and limit recovery.
- Electrophysiological findings: Preserved evoked potentials and motor-evoked potentials preoperatively predict better functional recovery—these suggest intact axonal conduction despite clinical deficits.

Timing of decompression is crucial. Because early ischemic and demyelinating changes are amenable to reversal, prompt restoration of normal pressure and flow dynamics yields the best chance of meaningful neurological improvement. Conversely, once neuronal loss and dense gliosis are established, expect limited restitution of function.

# Animal models and experimental insights into syringogenesis

Experimental animal models have been indispensable for dissecting the mechanisms of syrinx formation and testing interventions. Models have used mechanical obstruction, syrinx induction by fluid injection, chronic compression, and genetic or developmental manipulation to reproduce features of human syringomyelia.

Classic models include:

- Craniospinal CSF obstruction models: Insertion of a small mass or adhesive at the foramen magnum or creation of arachnoid scarring in cats, dogs, and rodents produces altered CSF flow and, in many cases, syrinx formation downstream in the spinal cord. These models support the role of impaired subarachnoid CSF dynamics and transmit the piston mechanism of tonsillar descent in syrinx genesis.

- Pressure-pulse models: Controlled application of pulsatile pressure to subarachnoid spaces has induced syrinxes, indicating that transmission of high-amplitude pulsations into the cord is sufficient to drive cavity formation, particularly when accompanied by perivascular pathway access.

- Intramedullary injection models: Injection of small volumes of fluid or blood products directly into the cord parenchyma can create cystic cavities that evolve into syrinx-like structures, highlighting the role of ependymal disruption and local tissue necrosis. These models also reproduce gliotic margins and demyelination seen in human syringes.

- Tethering and traction models: Spinal cord tethering models show that mechanical tension can alter CSF flow and promote cavitation, suggesting that mechanical stretch and altered tissue mechanics contribute to syringogenesis.

Key insights from animal studies:

- Perivascular routes are primary conduits: Tracer studies demonstrate that fluid introduced into the subarachnoid space can enter the spinal cord parenchyma via perivascular spaces, lending strong evidence to models positing that perivascular flow is central to syrinx development.

- Ependymal disruption is critical: Animals with preserved ependyma and normal subarachnoid flow rarely form syrinxes, even when subjected to elevated pulsatile pressure. Conversely, deliberate ependymal injury greatly increases syrinx risk.

- Role of aquaporin-4: Experimental modulation of aquaporin-4 expression alters interstitial fluid movement and can modify syrinx progression. Knockout or pharmacologic modulation in rodents indicates that aquaporin-mediated water transport contributes to cavity dynamics.

- Reversibility depends on timing: Early decompression in animal models often leads to syrinx regression and functional improvement, mirroring clinical observations. Delayed intervention yields persistent cavities lined by gliosis and limited recovery.

- Inflammation and scarring modulate outcomes: Models that introduce inflammatory components (e.g., blood products into the subarachnoid space) demonstrate increased arachnoid scarring, disrupted CSF flow, and a higher propensity for syringogenesis, reinforcing the clinical linkage between meningitis/hemorrhage and syrinx formation.

Limitations of animal models include species differences in CSF dynamics, cord size and compliance, and relative paucity of congenital Chiari analogs. Nevertheless, these models clarify mechanisms—particularly the interplay of pulsatile forces, perivascular pathways, ependymal integrity, and astrocytic responses—and they provide preclinical platforms for testing interventions such as syrinx shunting, arachnoid dissection techniques, and molecular modulators of fluid transport.

Conclusion

Neuropathology of chronic brainstem compression and syrinx formation reflects an integrated process of mechanical stress, vascular compromise, fluid dynamics, and secondary degenerative change. Ischemia, venous congestion, gliosis, demyelination, neuronal loss, and ependymal disruption compose the histopathologic spectrum that determines clinical manifestations affecting respiratory control, cranial nerve function, and spinal cord systems. Clinical recovery after decompression is best when intervention occurs before irreversible neuronal loss and dense gliosis have developed; animal models affirm the central roles of perivascular flow and ependymal integrity in syringogenesis and offer avenues for therapeutic innovation. A mechanistic appreciation of these processes enhances clinical reasoning about the natural history, imaging findings, and prognosis of patients with brainstem compression and syringomyelia.

# Introduction

Chiari I malformation (CM-I) is defined by downward herniation of the cerebellar tonsils through the foramen magnum. It can be an incidental imaging finding or a cause of significant neurologic morbidity through hindbrain compression, impairment of cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) flow at the craniocervical junction, and associated syringomyelia. Neurosurgical management is focused on restoring CSF flow, decompressing neural structures, and treating or preventing secondary syrinx formation while minimizing surgical morbidity. This chapter reviews the clinical and radiographic indications for posterior fossa decompression (PFD), the operative techniques commonly used (suboccipital craniectomy, C1 laminectomy, and duraplasty choices), the role of CSF flow studies, approaches to syringomyelia associated with CM-I, anticipated complications and their management, and expected outcomes for headache, focal neurologic deficits, and sleep-respiratory disorders.

# Clinical and radiographic indications for posterior fossa decompression

Indications for surgery in CM-I are primarily clinical. The classic scenario prompting neurosurgical consideration is symptomatic patients with signs attributable to hindbrain compression or CSF-flow obstruction. Typical symptoms include occipital or suboccipital Valsalva-related headaches (cough, sneeze, or strain-provoked), progressive myelopathic signs (weakness, sensory dissociation, spasticity), long-tract findings, lower cranial nerve dysfunction (dysphagia, dysarthria), and progressive scoliosis in children. In infants and young children, failure to thrive, apnea, or bulbar dysfunction may be presenting features. Symptomatic syringomyelia—neuropathic pain, dissociated sensory loss, or progressive weakness—constitutes another strong indication for decompression.

Radiographic findings inform but do not dictate management. There is no absolute radiologic threshold that mandates surgery without correlated symptoms. Several commonly used MRI criteria:

- Degree of hindbrain herniation: cerebellar tonsil descent below the foramen magnum of greater than 5 mm is frequently used to define CM-I in adults. However, symptomatic correlation is imperfect; smaller degrees of descent can be symptomatic, and larger herniations may be incidental.
- Evidence of CSF flow obstruction at the foramen magnum, demonstrated as obliteration of the subarachnoid space on T2-weighted MRI or as abnormal flow dynamics on cine phase-contrast MRI.
- Presence of a syringomyelia/syrinx: a syrinx that is expanding, causing neurologic deficit, or is associated with progressive scoliosis in pediatric patients is a strong indication for surgical intervention directed at restoring CSF flow.
- Associated craniovertebral junction anomalies (e.g., basilar invagination, atlantoaxial instability), which may require tailored operative strategies addressing bony instability rather than or in addition to standard decompression.

Decision points hinge on symptom severity, progression, and correlation with imaging. Asymptomatic patients with incidental tonsillar ectopia are generally observed. Those with mild symptoms and no syrinx may be managed conservatively with monitoring unless symptoms progress. Progressive neurologic deficits, clear Valsalva headaches affecting quality of life, and syringomyelia with neurologic compromise are accepted indications for posterior fossa decompression.

# Surgical approaches: suboccipital craniectomy, C1 laminectomy, duraplasty options

The fundamental goals of surgical approaches are to enlarge the posterior fossa outlet, restore normal CSF pathways at the foramen magnum, and relieve direct tonsillar compression while minimizing disruption to normal structures.

Suboccipital craniectomy and C1 laminectomy: The most common approach combines a suboccipital craniectomy with a C1 laminectomy. The craniectomy involves removal of part or all of the suboccipital bone overlying the foramen magnum and low cerebellar hemispheres. Extent varies: a limited midline suboccipital burr hole enlarged to a craniectomy measuring roughly 2–4 cm in transverse diameter is frequently sufficient; larger craniectomies risk destabilization and cerebellar sag. Key landmarks are the inion superiorly, the foramen magnum inferiorly, and the occipital condyles laterally. C1 laminectomy extends the decompression caudally and may be partial or complete depending on tonsillar descent; removal of the posterior arch of C1 increases the subarachnoid cross-sectional area and relieves mechanical compression.

Bone-only (bony decompression) versus dural opening: Some surgeons perform only a bony decompression (bone-only PFD), leaving the dura intact, while others open the dura and perform an expansile duraplasty. Bone-only decompression decreases operative time and theoretically reduces the risk of CSF-related complications, but it may be less effective when intradural factors—tight arachnoidal adhesions, crowded tonsils—contribute to CSF obstruction. Duraplasty is associated with higher rates of syrinx resolution and symptomatic improvement in many series but also carries higher rates of CSF leak and wound-related complications.

Duraplasty techniques: When the dura is opened, the goal is an expansile duraplasty that enlarges the cisterna magna and recreates normal CSF flow. Steps include midline durotomy (or Y-shaped incision), inspection of the subarachnoid space, lysis of arachnoid adhesions if present, and patching of the dura with a graft. Options for dural graft material:

- Autologous tissues: pericranium or fascia lata. Advantages: low immunogenicity, resistance to infection; disadvantages: additional incision to harvest (fascia lata) and variable graft size.
- Allografts/xenografts: bovine pericardium or cadaveric dura (used less commonly). Ease of use but theoretical risk of infection or rejection.
- Synthetic grafts: expanded polytetrafluoroethylene (ePTFE), collagen matrices. Readily available and consistent, but some series report higher CSF leak rates.

Graft selection depends on surgeon preference, availability, and anticipated need for watertight closure. Technique for closure includes meticulous suturing, often augmented with fibrin sealant and layered soft-tissue closure to minimize CSF leak.

Intradural maneuvers: After durotomy, assessment of the cerebellar tonsils and subarachnoid spaces is performed. Two principal options exist for addressing the tonsils intradurally: conservative coagulation/contouring (tonsillar cautery or bipolar shrinkage) and partial subpial resection (tonsillar reduction). Coagulation reduces tonsillar bulk with less manipulation; resection provides more decompression but increases risk of cerebellar injury, hemorrhage, and cranial nerve dysfunction. Lysis of arachnoid adhesions between the tonsils and brainstem/spinal cord and widening of the foramen of Magendie may be performed to re-establish normal CSF pathways.

Adjunct stabilization: If craniocervical instability or basilar invagination is present, posterior fusion and/or ventral decompression may be necessary. Preoperative dynamic radiographs and CT are key to identifying instability that would mandate fusion in addition to decompression.

# Role of intraoperative and preoperative CSF flow studies

Cine phase-contrast MRI (PC-MRI) is the main preoperative imaging modality used to evaluate CSF dynamics at the craniocervical junction. It quantifies pulsatile CSF flow during the cardiac cycle and identifies flow obstructions, turbulent flow, or lack of flow across the foramen magnum. Preoperative PC-MRI can help select patients who are likely to benefit from decompression—patients with impaired anteroposterior CSF flow and CSF stasis are more likely to have symptomatic improvement and syrinx resolution after surgery. However, PC-MRI is not uniformly required; clinical correlation remains paramount.

Intraoperative assessment of CSF flow can be performed subjectively by the surgeon after durotomy and tonsillar decompression—visualization of restored pulsatile CSF movement in the cisterna magna and around the foramen magnum is reassuring. More objective intraoperative methods include ultrasound to observe cisternal patency or intraoperative Doppler/flow probes, but these are less commonly used. Some centers use intraoperative phase-contrast MRI or real-time ultrasound to ensure adequate decompression before dural closure; such technologies increase operative time and resource utilization.

Interpretation and limitations: PC-MRI findings must be interpreted with clinical context. Small residual flow abnormalities can persist without symptoms, and normalization of PC-MRI after surgery does not guarantee clinical improvement. Conversely, some patients with normal preoperative PC-MRI may still benefit from decompression due to local compression or dynamic factors not captured by a single study. Thus PC-MRI is a valuable adjunct for surgical planning, patient counseling, and postoperative assessment but is not an absolute gatekeeper for surgery.

# Techniques for addressing associated syringomyelia (syrinx shunting vs decompression)

Syringomyelia associated with CM-I is thought to result primarily from obstruction of CSF flow at the foramen magnum, causing altered pressure dynamics and generation/propagation of a syrinx within the spinal cord. The first-line neurosurgical approach for CM-I–associated syringomyelia is posterior fossa decompression aimed at restoring normal CSF flow; in many patients the syrinx collapses or decreases in size over months after successful decompression.

Posterior fossa decompression with duraplasty: When successful in re-establishing CSF pulsations and cisternal patency, PFD with duraplasty leads to syrinx reduction in a majority of patients. Predictors of a good response include restoration of CSF flow across the foramen magnum, absence of intradural arachnoid scarring that cannot be adequately relieved, and shorter duration of syrinx-related symptoms.

Syrinx shunting: Direct syrinx shunting (for example, syringoperitoneal, syringosubarachnoid, or syringopleural shunts) diverts syrinx fluid directly from the cavity to another compartment. Indications include:

- Failure of syrinx reduction after adequate decompression and persistent/recurrent neurologic deficits attributable to the syrinx.
- Complex syrinx anatomy that is unlikely to resolve with decompression alone (e.g., syrinx with a thick arachnoid septation preventing collapse).
- Poor surgical candidate for reoperation at the posterior fossa but requiring syrinx decompression for progressive myelopathy.

Shunt types have different profiles. Syringoperitoneal shunts are commonly used because the peritoneum absorbs CSF reliably; a catheter is placed into the syrinx cavity with a distal catheter tunneled to the peritoneal cavity and connected to a valve system if desired. Syringosubarachnoid shunts attempt to reestablish communication between the syrinx and subarachnoid space by placing a catheter from the syrinx into the subarachnoid space; this carries a risk of arachnoid scarring and failure. Choice of shunt depends on anatomy, surgeon experience, and prior operations.

Risks of syrinx shunting include shunt malfunction, infection, catheter migration, overdrainage with resultant spinal cord collapse or arachnoiditis, and persistent neurological deficits. Because shunt procedures do not correct the primary obstruction at the craniocervical junction, they may be less durable than successful decompression in appropriate patients.

Decision algorithm: Current practice favors initial PFD with duraplasty for CM-I with syrinx unless clear reasons exist to proceed directly to shunting. If syrinx persists or progresses despite adequate PFD and CSF flow restoration, or if prompt syrinx drainage is needed to arrest progressive myelopathy, syrinx shunting can be considered as salvage therapy.

# Complications: CSF leak, infection, posterior fossa haematoma, cranial nerve injury

Posterior fossa surgery has specific complications related to the location and the nature of the decompression. Understanding likely complications and their management is critical.

CSF leak and pseudomeningocele: CSF leak is one of the most common complications, particularly when the dura is opened and a duraplasty is performed. Reported rates vary widely (approximately 2–15% in many series), influenced by graft material, technique, and patient factors. Clinical presentation includes wound drainage, subcutaneous fluid collection (pseudomeningocele), headache, and sometimes meningitis. Management begins with conservative measures: bed rest, head elevation, lumbar drainage may reduce CSF pressure and allow healing, and external CSF diversion is sometimes used. Persistent leaks or wound problems typically require operative repair with revision of the dural closure and reinforcement with autologous grafts and soft-tissue coverage.

Infection: Risk of superficial wound infection or deep central nervous system infection (meningitis, cerebellar abscess) exists. Meticulous sterile technique, perioperative antibiotics per institutional protocol, and prompt recognition of wound erythema or drainage are essential. Treatment ranges from antibiotics for superficial infections to operative washout for deep infections, removal of infected graft material when necessary, and targeted antibiotic therapy.

Posterior fossa hematoma: Hemorrhage in the posterior fossa is a feared complication because of the limited expansile capacity of the posterior fossa and the risk for rapid brainstem compression. Presentation can be delayed or immediate and includes decreased consciousness, new cranial nerve deficits, or rapid neurologic deterioration. Preventive measures include meticulous hemostasis, blood pressure control, and careful coagulation of bleeding points. Diagnosis is by urgent CT or MRI; management requires prompt neurosurgical intervention—usually evacuation of the hematoma and external ventricular drainage if hydrocephalus is present.

Cranial nerve injury and bulbar dysfunction: Lower cranial nerve (CN IX–XII) dysfunction may occur from direct manipulation, ischemia, edema, or postoperative scarring. Manifestations include dysphagia, dysarthria, aspiration, and loss of gag reflex. Intraoperative monitoring of cranial nerve function (brainstem auditory evoked potentials, somatosensory evoked potentials, and lower cranial nerve monitoring when available) may reduce risk. Management includes supportive care, swallow evaluations, temporary feeding support (nasogastric tube or gastrostomy if prolonged), speech therapy, and in rare cases, surgical revision if compressive lesions such as hematoma are identified.

Other complications: Hydrocephalus can occur postoperatively due to impaired CSF absorption or obstruction of outflow; it may require ventricular drainage or shunting. Cerebellar mutism and ataxia are rare but possible, particularly with excessive cerebellar retraction or injury. Spinal instability is a theoretic risk if excessive bone removal extends to the occipital condyles; therefore, preoperative assessment of craniovertebral junction stability guides how aggressive bony resection should be.

# Outcomes for headache, neurological deficits and sleep-respiratory abnormalities

Outcomes after PFD for CM-I are variable and correlate with preoperative symptom type and chronicity, presence of syrinx, adequacy of decompression, and patient factors. Expectations must be individualized and communicated clearly to patients and families.

Headache: Valsalva-related occipital headaches are among the symptoms most likely to improve after successful decompression. Reported rates of improvement vary but many series cite symptomatic improvement in 60–90% of patients with appropriately selected headaches. However, nonspecific chronic headaches or migrainous phenotypes less reliably respond. Predictors of good outcome include classic cough-related occipital headaches, radiographic evidence of CSF flow obstruction, and shorter symptom duration before surgery.

Neurological deficits and syrinx-related symptoms: Motor weakness, sensory disturbances, and pain related to syringomyelia often improve after decompression, particularly when surgery is performed early in the course of neurologic decline. Degree of improvement is greater for pain and sensory symptoms than for long-standing motor weakness or severe myelopathy. Syrinx size reduction is seen on MRI in many patients—reports commonly show syrinx collapse or significant reduction in 50–80% of properly selected cases. Persistent deficits may reflect irreversible spinal cord injury, especially when symptoms were longstanding or the syrinx was large. Long-term stabilization of neurologic function is a realistic goal, with improvement more likely when decompression restores CSF flow (as confirmed by PC-MRI) and when reoperation is not required.

Sleep-respiratory abnormalities: Central sleep apnea and sleep-disordered breathing can be associated with CM-I, particularly when there is brainstem compression. Improvement in sleep-respiratory symptoms after PFD has been reported, but responses are variable. Patients with central apnea and documented brainstem compression may show significant improvement in respiratory events and oxygenation after decompression, but obstructive sleep apnea (OSA) related to upper airway anatomy is less likely to improve. Preoperative polysomnography and postoperative reassessment are important for objective evaluation. Patients with severe sleep-respiratory compromise may require perioperative respiratory support and careful monitoring.

Long-term outcomes and reoperation: Recurrence of symptoms or radiographic progression can occur, sometimes related to inadequate decompression, graft failure with re-adhesion, or unrecognized craniocervical instability. Reoperation rates vary; when reoperation is necessary, options include revision decompression with duraplasty, arachnolysis, or posterior fusion if instability is identified. Patient counseling should include discussion of the possibility of reoperation and lifelong follow-up in some cases.

Quantitative outcome ranges: Because studies use heterogeneous criteria and follow-up intervals, precise universal probabilities are not available. As an approximate guide: symptomatic improvement rates after PFD for classical CM-I range 60–85% for Valsalva headaches, 50–80% for syrinx-related pain and sensory symptoms, and 30–70% for motor weakness depending on chronicity.
